ParlaMint-NL_2017-02-08-tweedekamer-10.u1	I would like to welcome the Minister of the Interior and Kingdom Relations and the Minister of Defence, as well as the people in the public gallery and the MPs. I am the first to give the floor to Mr Verhoeven on behalf of D66. Mr Verhoeven has spoken for 75 minutes.
ParlaMint-NL_2017-02-08-tweedekamer-10.u2	Mr. Chairman. The world is changing. We live and communicate more and more digitally. It creates opportunities, but it also creates risks. We already saw this in the Computer Crime Act III, also known as the hacking law. We dealt with them a few weeks ago. We recently saw this in the vulnerabilities in the election software, not to mention the use of private mail by this cabinet. Today, the bill for the new Intelligence and Security Services Act is before us. That law regulates what the AIVD and the STUDY can and cannot do. The bill regulates both new powers and a change of supervision. Clearly, I have a lot of concerns about this bill. Is this law effective? Does this law make the Netherlands safer? Are all new powers also surrounded by good guarantees? What are the consequences for the economy? My group's 27 pages in the preparatory round, my 20 amendments, which I will explain later, and the 75 minutes I have requested, of course, have given a hint that D66 is concerned about this law. I rarely ask for such a long speaking time, Madam President, you know that. In the last six years, I have only done this once before, that hacking law I was talking about just before Christmas. Today, too, I would like to use enough time to explain precisely and nuancedly where my group's concerns lie about this bill and how it can be removed. I have noticed in recent years and certainly in recent weeks how many people are afraid of a dragnet. The Minister of the Interior, Minister Plasterk, even received the Big Brother Award for it. For this reason, too, I think it is important to speak extensively so that everyone following the debate, at home or here in the public gallery, also sees that Parliament is treating this far-reaching law seriously, because it is a very far-reaching law, let us not forget that. It is also important that the questions and concerns that live with many are properly addressed. Those questions and concerns came to us in large numbers via email and social media. Many people ask us what will happen to this law and deserve a serious answer. For this reason, too, I hope that I will be able to convince my colleagues and the two ministers in trade union K with my input with substantive arguments that the law needs to be adapted to key issues. I first express a word of appreciation for the two services, namely the AIVD and the STUDY. They work hard and meticulously to keep the Netherlands and our soldiers safe on mission. They can't or hardly claim their successes, but misses have great consequences. They therefore have a difficult position in which they have to find the balance between security, effectiveness and fundamental rights over and over again, but they seem to succeed in taking that position. Where in the news one intelligence scandal follows after another, especially about British services and American services, the Dutch services keep to the law. That's what the monitoring report shows. It is only occasional that an illegality is found and therefore not systematically or on a large scale. That is why all these people deserve a great compliment, although this does not mean giving them unbridled powers without control. When presenting this bill, the government made an infographic "The New Reality" which bore the title "The New Reality" from the first search engine in 1993 to the 1 billion websites the Internet had estimated in 2014. Behind that infographic is, of course, a much broader story, namely that of a society that is increasingly digitalising. ICT is increasingly important in people's lives, namely for communication, for the search and storage of information and for leisure activities. Think of Netflix and YouTube. But it is also important for our work, in which we store sensitive business information. There is almost no longer any aspect of our lives to think of that digitalisation does not matter. That indicates the enormous importance of this digital technology. This importance will only increase further in the coming years. Houses become SMART with internet-connected devices. Production methods become digital with robots and 3D printers. Cars are already computers on wheels and with self-driving cars the importance of digital technology is only increasing further. These are developments that offer enormous opportunities for greater convenience in our daily lives, for saving energy and for economic growth and jobs. I stress this enormous importance and the opportunities of ICT in our society for a reason. I do this because too often we only look at the need for detection of a measure. When it comes to ICT and its complexity, the House of Representatives often agrees too quickly and too easily with all kinds of measures that are very damaging to our freedom and our economy and that can even harm our security, simply because one does not understand the overall impact and thus cannot see the consequences. Take the example of the Gaming Law. It first included the power to block websites via so-called dns-blocks. That was a completely useless power that was proposed by this government and would not be safer for people. It would, however, restrict people's freedom and cause economic damage. Fortunately, D66 has been able to pull that power out of that bill, but this shows that the cabinet wants to move too quickly and unthinkingly towards new digital powers. It sounds very tough and simple, but it often doesn't work. Of course, this does not mean that we should be blind to the risks of increased use of ICT. For example, terrorists are now communicating differently than before: cyber threats are rapidly increasing. The cabinet says that's why this law is needed. Of course, it is not surprising that the government continues to search the digital network for ways to keep people safe, because that is its job and the Netherlands is not unique in this respect: throughout Europe and even throughout the world, we see governments trying to get more control over the Internet, because they want to try to protect people. But that often has major consequences for people's economy and privacy, and unfortunately too often it doesn't really work. Take Snowden's revelations that U.S. and British intelligence were collecting uncompromisingly huge amounts of data and information, that they were actively trying to undermine encryption and hacking people on a large scale. Of course, that was a major violation of the privacy of 100 million people. It affected people's online security and cost US technology companies billions, but above all, these revelations damaged people's confidence in the intelligence services, including the Dutch. Now that trust is damaged, it doesn't help if the head of the AIVD publicly pleads for weakening encryption, which was one of the revelations people were so shocked about. In addition, it would seriously damage people's online safety. This is another example in which the internet and measures on the Internet are looked at solely from the point of view of research and intelligence policy. The same applies when a former head of the STUDY asks what kind of privacy you have when you're dead. This creates little confidence in the way the services, or at least the top people of those services, think about privacy. Is the Minister aware that confidence in the services has been damaged in recent years? This was not only due to the Snowden revelations, but also to the statements of the heads of our services. Does the Minister understand that these kinds of statements are counterproductive to the acceptance of new powers? What's the Minister gonna do to regain that trust? In making new laws and regulations, we should not just look at the need for detection. In the hacking law, I also advocated a broader consideration of the consequences of new powers, not only of the search interests of the services, which are in fact always at the forefront, sometimes as a single interest, but also of the impact on people's online safety, on people's online freedom, on the impact on our economy, on our democracy and on our fundamental rights. Of course, it's nice to be able to hack from the police, but it's our job as a Chamber to also see the consequences for society as a whole. For example, this consideration has been made very well in the cabinet's view on encryption. I have often congratulated the cabinet on this. The social and economic importance and security of encryption have been carefully considered, after which the assessment has been made that the weakening of encryption is not desirable, given the importance of encryption for security on the Internet, for the protection of people's privacy and for the confidential communication of government and companies, and therefore for the Dutch economy. That is the kind of broader consideration we need to make today when dealing with this law. D66 wants an open, free and secure internet. We have always shown this in our contribution to parliamentary debates and in the handling of laws, and that will also be the approach of D66 today. The Internet offers huge opportunities for society, for the economy, for people's personal development and also for bringing people in contact around the world. In that sense, we are also simply a party that stands for the individual freedom of people. ICT and the Internet can enhance individual freedom, but people only use the Internet and all these new technologies if they are sure that they can trust them. When everything people do is recorded, stored and analyzed, people's confidence in the Internet erodes. This will eventually lead to a situation in which people will seek an alternative, with all the consequences. That is what we want to prevent. Would people have started using letters or phones if they had known that all communication would be intercepted, stored and analyzed? So much for my general introduction. I shall now move on to the law, because most of this has to be said today. From the developments in the world and my vision of freedom and security in a digital world, I now come to the law that we are discussing here today. The biggest difference from the current law of 2002 lies in the extension of powers. From now on, not only the ether, the air, can be widely tapped, but also the communication about Internet cables. Research-oriented, the cabinet calls it in law. It's popularly called a dragnet. Also the previously started DNA database............................................................................................................................................................... Monitoring is also changing. In order to use the more profound, heavier, special powers, from now on, the Review Committee on Budgetary Powers, I call it the TIB Committee, to assess whether the ministerial consent, which must therefore be given by both ministers in each application for special powers, has been rightly granted. In addition, the CTIVD, the other supervisor we already know, a supervisor who mainly looks at the handling of the services afterwards, makes complaints. If we can believe the government, this law must prevent hell and damnation. The current law on intelligence and security services of 2002 would seriously entangle the AIVD and the STIVD. What, according to the government, is not possible under the current law? Identifying cyber-threat and terrorist activities in a timely manner. Protect Dutch military on mission well. Evaluate the real intentions of risk countries. Preventing economic espionage. Crises abroad well estimate. I think it's all a big deal. If it is really so serious and so urgent, why hasn't a review of this law been carried out sooner? Digitisation has been taking place for decades. What is the first moment when this cabinet received signals from their services that technological developments were hindering their work? Why breed three years on the bill and then ask the House of Representatives to deal with it quickly before the elections in a few months? I'd like a public reaction later, and not always that whispering on the left. Changes to the Constitution to modernise letter secrecy into a communication secret have already begun. In this way, a good, timely constitutional framework could have been created for the new Intelligence and Security Services Act. That would have been possible if the government had not paused that process of amending the Constitution to consider this law first. That order continues to amaze my group. I hope, however, that a proposal for the inclusion of a communication secret in the Constitution is well thought out and that the handling of this law will not lead to new insights. What other conclusion can I draw? For example, was the government afraid that a new law would conflict with the Constitution and would it prefer to adapt the Constitution to the law rather than the other way around, namely to adapt it to the Constitution? Do the ministers concerned understand that this is an undesirable sham solution and that this could damage confidence in the law and the services? I come to the content of the law, taking into account the five main themes for D66 in this law. I have built up this long speech in such a way that everyone can follow exactly what I have to say. I have five main issues on which the law needs to be improved: no dragnet, strong supervision, no legal shortcuts, responsible data analyses and better consideration for cooperating services from abroad. I will then deal with the amendments which are not covered by these five issues, and then I will end with a series of separate, but certainly no less important, questions. No dragnet. Anyone who has been following the news over the last few days, who is looking at the responses to the internet consultations and reading the input of all the experts for the round table, or who is seeing the messages from all kinds of interest groups and human rights organisations flow, can come to a conclusion: the fear of a dragnet with all the Internet traffic of everyone is great. Who you call, what you appt, what sites you visit, how healthy you are, it could all end up as a by-catch to the services. This could be another choice that different parties make here in a different way. It is a choice that is often too simplistic as security versus freedom. As if civil protection and privacy could not go together, as if privacy itself does not need to be protected. If it were a trade-off that should lead to a choice, this trade-off should be based on the principle that large-scale tapping makes us safer. But that's just not certain, because mass surveillance increases the datahooiberg and this law even makes the datahooiberg huge and that makes the pin harder to find. Old-NSA Director Bill Binney I think I have already mentioned this: bulk data kills people. In other words, with massive surveillance and collecting and storing all kinds of data, you don't save people's lives. Last weekend there was a good article in Het Financieele Dagblad about four risks of big data. One of these risks is that one starts to reason from the data rather than from a real problem. Insights that emerge from the data then become leading. That leads to ignoring real threats. The collection of data quickly becomes an end in itself instead of a means. One of the chapters in the explanatory memorandum of this law is called: the threat we do not know. On the one hand, it is precisely the duty of the services to identify the threat we do not know; on the other hand, it is also easy, and that is what is threatening to happen here, to become obsessed with the threat we do not know. The result is that disproportionate resources, I say to this cabinet, are being used to identify possible new threats. Instead of dealing with known threats, collecting large amounts of data becomes the leading goal. Collecting large amounts of data offers the false promise that all unknown threats can be identified in time. But that's a false promise, a mirage in a dry desert. The hard truth is that 100% security is not possible, even in most controlled police states. I am convinced that the services will work much more effectively if they do their job on the basis of concrete information. Concrete information from society, from ordinary people, district agents and civil society organisations. I want to illustrate that with the story of Rezwan Ferdaus. Rezwan was born and raised in Ashland, Massachusetts, a beautiful green suburb of Boston. His parents had emigrated from Bangladesh to the United States. He was a good student and graduated from Northeastern University in physics. When he couldn't find a job, he moved back to his parents and started spending a lot of time online. At some point he began to visit radical Islamic websites and was soon inspired by Al-Qaeda. At some point, he reached out to members of Al-Qaeda through his mosque to commit an attack in America. He made a plan and bought with a PayPal account under a false name a number of drones, replicas of fighter jets, including jet engine, that could fly at 250 kilometers per hour, and carry 20 kilos of explosives. The drones could take off almost anywhere and couldn't be detected by radar. His contact at the mosque resulted in 12 kilos of C4 explosions, a number of hand grenades and an A-47 rifle. With these drones full of explosives, Rezwan wanted to attack the parliament building and the Pentagon. At the same time, two teams of three people fleeing would try to shoot down the two targets. Rezwan had drawn up a hugely complex mission plan with aircraft specifications and software protocols. He had built robotics into the drones, taken maps and photos of the targets and put together a budget for the whole plan. Rezwan filed this plan with his contact at the mosque. Shortly after, he was given the green light to carry out the plan. On September 28, 2011, Rezwan travelled to a storage facility, where we had stored all the necessary equipment. That's where he was overwhelmed by the FBI agents. Turned out what? The person he had approached at the mosque to put him in touch with Al-Qaeda was right to call the police. All of his contacts, which were supposedly Al-Qaeda members, were in fact FBI agents. However, it was not the NSA dragnet that noticed Rezwan's plans and it had not happened thanks to weakened encryption protocols or expensive hacking software. No, it was just an honest Islamic citizen who noticed Rezwan's plans and went to the police. The government seems to recognise this as well, because in response to my questions the remark was made that a towing network would be 'hidden' again. It is also legal that informationism is not easy to stop, especially if the law leaves room for it and data storage is becoming cheaper and cheaper. The law must therefore provide a good guarantee. The law itself must provide a good guarantee, not just supervision, not just the way we organized everything around the law. The law itself must also provide a guarantee against that snooping. At the heart of the question is then: how selective is this key term "research-oriented"? Opponents of the law read it as: very unfocused. Proponents say, no, it's very precise. I don't know how to read it yet. I have read the text of the law, the Minister's answers, all the explanations and opinions very well, but I do not know how to read the word'research-oriented'. The cabinet simply explains it very cumbersomely. Earlier I asked the cabinet if certain possibilities could be excluded, for example the interception, tapping, all communication in a certain Dutch neighborhood or neighbourhood for a certain period of time in order to see if relevant data would have been retrieved for the services. The answer was as follows: that in no case can this meet the legal requirements of proportionality and subsidiarity. And: that a commitment is excluded for such reasons. The Minister clicks... I mean nod. I hope he doesn't click, but he keeps nodding when I'm talking. He certainly says that this kind of hypothetical way of tapping, so the large-scale unfocused tapping of an entire neighborhood, is not possible in this law. For me, then, the question remains as to what reason it is not excluded in the law. This is stated in the note on the basis of the report, in the written answers to questions put by the Court. Why isn't it ruled out in the law? When is the acquisition, processing or analysis of all communication in a Dutch neighborhood or neighbourhood justified for a certain period of time? After all, if the situation was not foreseeable, why include the shortcut in the answer that a commitment is excluded for these reasons only, rather than exclusion for all reasons? What guarantee is there in the law itself and for the total number of research assignments? A single research assignment may be limited in scope and time, but that does not mean that the whole of research is as well. How does the Minister view this relationship? What guarantee of that is there in the law now? I think this guarantee is missing. The legality judgements focus on the person or organisation concerned, not on the by-catch and certainly not on all other use of powers. That is why I have tabled an amendment. That was submitted in document No. 34. That amendment does two things. First of all, it is stated that the use of the powers provided for in Articles 48, 49 and 50 in the draft law for research-oriented acquisition, processing and analysis of communication data is as targeted as possible. Secondly, that amendment defines that a research mission or the whole of research assignments is by definition not selective enough if this leads to the systematic monitoring of persons who are not themselves subject to research on a large scale. I would like to respond to this amendment. The amendment is in fact perfectly in line with what the Minister is suggesting to respond to a critical question from D66 in the written input. The amendment should therefore be merely a hammer piece. With the possible acceptance of this amendment, not all the problems have been solved, because the directive on selectivity is there, but is it actually happening? For years, our services have been having problems with the motivation and selection criteria for the diagnostic list in the deployment of Signals Intelligence, the current power to tap the air unfocused. Then there are the questions "what characteristics can we look for when listening to satellite traffic?" and "Can these criteria actually pass the test of criticism?" Over and over again, there are unlawful judgments of our supervisor, the CTIVD. Is the new interception system, which also allows unfocused tapping of the cable, not going to cause the same problem? In fact, what was allowed on the air is now more or less possible on the cable. Then why should I suddenly have to trust that the motivation of the lists of selection terms for processing is now in order? I'd like to get a reaction to that. In the explanatory documents I read a lot about negative filtering, about constantly selecting data and constantly refining research assignments. I think that's an attempt to be reassuring. It also requires the so-called "select while you collect" principle, the selection of data, to be made compulsory. On that point, I have a list of questions for the ministers. How is it determined on which cable fiber the tap is needed? People talk about screenshots all the time, but can't the fiber about which the wanted communication is going to change all the time? How exactly is negative filtered? It is stated that Netflix, YouTube and the like are excluded. I think that's reassuring for many people, but aren't research objects and terrorist networks going to communicate via messages in videos? Is it true that negative filtered information is not acquired by the services? Is it true that they see that information passing through, but that it does not linger in the filters and that there is no registration, acquisition or knowledge whatsoever? One part of the "select while you collect" principle is the retention periods. That means: quickly and continuously select on relevance. It is therefore a continuing obligation to destroy. There is one point I would like to clarify. The data shall be immediately destroyed as soon as it is established that the data is not relevant. The government has added the term "before" in structural terms. I believe that this has been done in response to a comment from the SGP. The interception system, however, the search on the cable, is precisely aimed at filling out white spots, the so-called unknown unknowns. Then how do you determine that data is irrelevant? Is that not impossible and therefore a licence to retain data up to the maximum retention period? The maximum retention period is also longer than usual, much longer. In the case of data acquired from cable, the period of three years is not one year. If the data is encrypted, it may even go up to six years, because then it may also be used for another three years to decrypt the data. You'll have three plus three years, so that could be six years. Both the Council of State and the supervisor, the CTIVD, have doubts about this long retention period. My group, too, has serious doubts about this. I have therefore tabled an amendment which regulates the distinction recommended by the CTIVD between the retention period for the content of communication and the metadata, the so-called user rules. This is regulated in the amendment to item No. 27. For the content, the retention period shall be one year, as with the other powers. For metadata, my group understands the need for extension. These data are very useful to be able to look back in time, to reconstruct things and to discover new networks, but three years is, given the strong degree of privacy breach, still too long. We have therefore proposed that this period should be cut by half and thus brought back to one and a half years. That is still six months longer than with substantive data. By shortening the deadlines, there is automatically a brake on the amount of data collected. This is, moreover, no more than the services can analyse within the normal time frame. So that haystack, that huge bucket of data in which you can't find anything if you're not careful, remains clear and remains the pin we all want to look for, can be found. I come to my second point: supervision. In recent years, the CTIVD, the current supervisor, has built up a reputation as a powerful and reliable supervisor. This is a good thing for Parliament, because unlike other subjects, we often cannot obtain additional information. A lot's classified. This is also necessary, but the importance of national security is often at odds with the breach that secret services by definition make on people's lives and sometimes also with the possibility of parliamentary control. Those concerned, persons examined, often cannot appeal themselves, if they already know that they are being investigated or followed. The case law of the European Human Rights Court, the ECHR, shows that this has an impact on the requirements for monitoring those services. There should be an independent body, monitoring should extend to the various stages of the investigation and monitoring should be effective. Before the CTIVD, the University of Leiden analysed how the supervision of the intelligence and security services should be organised in order to meet this requirement. First of all, there must be an independent binding test prior to the use of a power. There must also be binding and independent ex post judicial supervision and binding and independent complaint procedures. On paper, the bill complies with the introduction of the TIB, the review committee, and with the move from the Minister's complaint handling to the CTIVD to all these three requirements, but will this actually lead to effective supervision in practice? I have a number of questions about this, first of all about the Review Committee on Betting Powers, the TIB. It will test many ministerial permissions in advance of the use of a special competence. The TIB does this with the same motivation and information as the minister, but without access to the systems of the services. That access has the CTIVD, the other supervisor, though. The Advisory Section of the Council of State expressed its concern that the Committee of Inquiry did not have "the necessary understanding and overview of the consequences of implementation practice and thus the proportionality of the use of the various powers." Simply put: the Council of State actually expects that the review committee will rarely refuse permission. In view of the safety risks in the event of a possible refusal, it will be taken into account in the event of doubt. In this case there is no effective supervision, but a stamping machine. I have to say that I am not entirely immune to that fear, but I also see opportunities to prevent the realization of that fear. That starts with experience and knowledge in the review committee. Unlike a supervisor afterwards, you can't gradually build up knowledge if you have to check prior consent. From the day this law enters into force, the TIB, the review committee, will therefore have to be there. How does the government prepare that? Who and which bodies are involved in the start-up of the TIB? Is there any insight into how many experienced judges have sufficient knowledge of this field? When are they gonna get a pulse on whether or not they're interested? How will it be prevented that all Members leave at once in six or twelve years' time? Fortunately, the letter of amendment already provides that there is no longer any distinction between judges within the case law and their colleagues at the Council of State, the Board of Appeal for Business and the Central Board of Appeal. I thank the cabinet for taking over this suggestion. That saves us another amendment and perhaps a few minutes of speaking time. More importantly, in this way we will remove any distinction and increase the pool of judges from which we can fish for a strong review committee. That doesn't mean we're here yet. One of the standards for good supervision, formulated by the Institute for Information Law and which I fully agree with, is the principle of contradiction. No plea should be made in a structural manner without opposition. We do not do that in the case law, we do not do that in the administrative discussions, and we certainly do not do that in the democratic processes. Then why is this happening in the supervision of the AIVD and the STIVD? The answer to the written questions showed that the government does not see any advantage in an audience lawyer, a lawyer who, by default, tries to put holes in the justifications submitted to the review committee, but I cannot imagine that government would be against an expert shell, a circle of knowledge, around the review committee. The committee could then seek advice from that group on questions of doubt and difficulty. That also brings a fresh look at the assessment of the ministerial consents. This is their day-to-day work for the members of the review committee. It is work that is always argued on one side. This simply creates a risk of identifying with the importance of services. It is then good to know that despite the confidentiality of secrecy, if our amendment is accepted, it is legally provided for in a club of people to whom the committee members can ask for advice and opposition. This may arise, for example, when a new technical application of a competence is envisaged. That is why I am voting in favour of this amendment. Thirty-six. The conditions to be met in order to be able to act as an expert and the remuneration may, moreover, be regulated by General Measure of Management. For example, the law provides scope, while lower legislation allows for the necessary organisation of, for example, incompatible functions, safety aspects and the like. I would also like to hear a response to this amendment. I have good news, Mr. Chairman, because I am well on schedule. I'm at half my speech and I've only spoken for 35 minutes. So I could be talking five minutes shorter than I thought. That is also for colleagues...
ParlaMint-NL_2017-02-08-tweedekamer-10.u3	They hang on your lips!
ParlaMint-NL_2017-02-08-tweedekamer-10.u4	Well, the most important thing now is for the Cabinet to be careful. What surprises me for some time is the way in which monitoring reports from the CTIVD, which is ex post supervision and which we already have, reach the Chamber. When a CTIVD report is ready, it first goes to the minister, not to parliament. The Minister then sends it to Parliament with a reaction. The Minister thus determines when the report becomes public, not the supervisor himself. In order to ensure that reports do not disappear in a drawer until a favourable moment for disclosure occurs, I am thinking of publication on a Friday evening or during a political crisis................................................................................................................................................................. My amendment to item No. 30 to that end. The Minister then remains obliged to respond, but does so after the publication of the report. That shouldn't take long, by the way. It is and will continue to be so arranged that the Minister is given the opportunity to respond before the report is adopted. Some time before the publication, the Minister will be familiar with the content of the report and will be able to work on his response. In this amendment, therefore, we take into account not only the transparency and control position of the Chamber, but also the workload and the possibility for the Cabinet to provide a careful response. What more do you want? Another concern is the relationship between the Review Committee and the CTIVD, i.e. between the existing ex post review supervisor and the new committee to review the Minister's consent. With the CTIVD we have an authorityal supervisor. However, some are concerned that its role is being undermined by the arrival of the new Review Committee, the TIB. The government also writes in the explanatory statement that the CTIVD is in principle bound by the legality judgment of the TIB. That sounds weird to me. Isn't there a fundamental difference between the scrutiny by both committees? Isn't the TIB's test the most comparable to an interim injunction? In fact, summary information is the basis for a rapid and complete review. And is the CTIVD test no longer a ground procedure? This means that we have access to all the systems and there is plenty of time for judgment. I'd like to hear a response to this equation. Of course, we must prevent the judgements of both committees from falling apart. Legal unit must be promoted as much as possible. As soon as there is room for manoeuvre and therefore there is no legal unit between the two committees, the services can make use of it. Since this is essential for effective ex-ante and ex-post monitoring, I make this explicit in an amendment. My text states that this is Amendment No D6607, but I do not know if that is true. In that amendment (34588), No. 39) it is in any case settled that the legal unit remains between the two committees. I would like to say a few words about the funding of both committees of inquiry. Finance is an important condition for effective supervision of services. The funds for this are invested in General Affairs. For example, a direct influence of the Ministry of the Interior, which includes the AIVD, and of the Defence Department, which includes the STUDY. Well, that's lucky to stay that way. At present, the CTIVD has a budget of EUR 1.5 million. The bill states that the CTIVD and the TIB together receive EUR 1 million. I wonder if this is enough. The public conversation that the Chamber had with the CTIVD in December indicated that the CTIVD faces a major challenge. As powers become more digital, it must also have more and more technical knowledge. In addition to giving concrete judgments of legality, it is also increasingly monitoring the system. It just costs capacity. This also brings with it investments and expertise. In addition, the Committee of Inquiry will of course not be able to handle it with just three members. The secretariat will have to have some substantial scope, not only with a secretary and a secretary, but also with some researchers, such as legal staff or technical experts. The CTIVD currently needs EUR 1.5 million. So I cannot imagine that EUR 2.5 million will be enough for CTIVD and TIB together. Can the Ministers explain what this sum of 2.5 million is based on for both committees of inquiry? How much support from CTIVD and TIB is included? Does the CTIVD itself consider this sufficient? And insofar as there is already a TIB in existence, does this committee consider that amount sufficient? Finally, I come to this'strong supervision' block of future funding. Are the ministers prepared to state somewhere that regulators will also be given more resources if AIVD and STUD are given more resources? If more resources are given to the services, this will normally lead to more activities requiring supervision. I have not tabled an amendment on this point, but I would like to announce that I am perhaps going to table a motion on this point. I shall, of course, wait for the answer of the rulers first. Then I go to my third point and that is: no detours, no legal shortcuts. It is important that we do not include in the law any shortcuts that create all kinds of opportunities for the services to do things that are said to be not intended at oral times or in secondary writings, but that it is not regulated in the law itself well, hard and clearly. I am particularly concerned with the use of hacking power, as everyone here in this Chamber knows, the AIVD and the STIVD are already allowed to hack, but an extension is being given to the power. I am also concerned about automated access to databases under Article 39 of the legislative proposal. First of all, let me say that I am pleased that the level of permission for hacking has been increased from head of service to minister. I think this also fits in with the increased importance of secure internet-connected devices for the daily lives of people, our economy and our society. I would also like to congratulate the Minister on his approach to this fundamental issue. There is quite a difference in the way this minister treats the risks of hacking compared to the Secretary of State for Security and Justice, who, with a rather embarrassing display, tried to convince the Chamber a few weeks ago that he did not know or used purchased hacking software of unknown vulnerabilities, whereas this is evidently the case. I am glad that this Minister of the Interior is talking about this with more knowledge. I do have some questions about the Minister's answers on the possible detours that can be taken in hacking. In the case of hacking my concerns lie in two areas, namely the extent of security and the hacking through third parties. We regularly hear the head of the AIVD in the media about the problems posed by the increased online security awareness for its service. Since the Snowden revelations, people have been paying more attention to the security of their online communications. Thus, more and more messaging apps encrypt the messages sent through them. At the same time, this new Intelligence and Security Services Act continues to have the power to demand the cooperation of anyone who has knowledge of encryption to decipher it. The explanatory documents state that this does not involve any obligations, for example to build back doors into soft or hardware or to weaken encryption. This is in itself reassuring, but such a fundamental issue must also be re-established in the legislative text itself, both for the awareness of this prohibition to the programmer who is nevertheless being asked, and for the supervisor to have a firm grip. My amendment to item No. 13 does exactly that. I accept, in fact, that this amendment can also count on a positive appreciation and assessment from Vocational Training. It is another amendment that lays down in the law what the Minister is actually suggesting is simply the intention, but which is not yet in the law. So please respond to that amendment. Then about security and the use of unknown vulnerabilities to hack. On this point, the Minister said earlier that the main rule is that significant vulnerabilities that harm users' interests on the Internet are reported, at least I quote "provided there are no legal arguments for protecting the current level of knowledge, working methods or sources to waive them'. What exactly is a significant vulnerability? Is it conceivable that the services withhold invulnerability in frequently used consumer software, for example a vulnerability to invade an iPhone? Does the Minister think that a found vulnerability in software used by millions of people every day and making them vulnerable to hacks by, for example, criminals or foreign intelligence services should always be reported? Among other things, in the debate on the hacking law, we have already noted as a Chamber, I believe that this was in an exchange of views with Mr Recourt of the PvdA, that this is a major dilemma. Do you keep an unknown vulnerability open so that you can hack into a terrorist at the same time as risking millions of people being hacked through the same vulnerability used by criminals or foreign intelligence services? Or are you going to close that vulnerability so millions of people are safe from hacks at risk of not hacking into a potential terrorist? Can the Minister provide an insight into how this consideration is being made? How are the importance of online security for people and the economic impact of keeping a vulnerability open? I would like to comment on this. I come to the amendment to item No. 48 about hacking through third parties. The Minister says that I would miss the fact that, in most cases, the services do not have direct physical access to the automated work of a target. I don't know how the minister got there. Can he explain that? Next, the Minister says that it is not desirable to limit the number of links between the automated work of a target and the services. But that is not at all what I am trying to do with my amendment. I do not know which amendment the Minister has read, but my amendment states that there must be a technical relationship, nothing more and nothing less. I would like a further response to this amendment. I'm here to consult informants. Anyone whose service expects him to have the necessary information may be contacted. This can be met by giving automated access to, for example, a database. Is it true that an informant can refuse to provide that information? What safeguards are in place against the imposition of undue pressure? How is it ensured that voluntary cooperation is actually voluntary? I can imagine that small companies or groups of people who depend on the government would not dare to say no so quickly. I ask this question because I can imagine that a service would rather go this way instead of hacking into the relevant database. If data retention of telecommunications is ever to be re-established, the cabinet is making an awful effort to do so, and I can imagine that it would be preferable to have access to the database of communication providers rather than to look for the right cable. That while both hacking and tapping are provided with more safeguards. So you take the path of the least resistance. How do these powers relate to each other, according to the Ministers? When are the services going to choose which authority? How do we prevent the services from hacking or tapping cables because they can ask for access to all possible databases? The fourth theme is the responsible data analysis. Communication is becoming more and more digital. Thanks to the new interception system, the services also have more and more data at home. We need to handle this digital data carefully, not only because of the privacy of the individuals about whom the services have information, but above all because of the effective performance of our tasks and thus for our national security. Part of this is reflected in the bill. It is therefore a question of ensuring that the promotion or adoption of measures against a person is not permitted solely on the basis of automated data analysis. For gourmets: this can be read in Article 60 (3). It seems very sensible. As I argued before, human considerations and sources appear to be the most effective. Data analysis can certainly support human considerations and sources, but cannot completely replace them. That is why we must first of all ensure that the quality of analysis and data processing is high and that the models and algorithms are reliable. To this end, I had the amendment on item No. 15, but the government has already taken this on board in the letter of amendment. On this point, I withdraw the amendment.

ParlaMint-NL_2017-02-08-tweedekamer-10.u6	I do have a number of questions about the way in which it is implemented. What standards does the government want to use for the models, algorithms, techniques and methods used for processing? What are the chances of error in that? What error margin in terms of false positives and false negatives does the government consider acceptable? How does this relate to other services in the Netherlands and abroad? I think there's another risk to the bill, and I think it's this. If services start to analyse automated data and are able to retrieve them on a large scale, there is a chance that a more or less complete picture of a person can be made. Just the user data of a mobile phone, that metadata, give a pretty intimate insight into someone's life. In fact, I think it is appropriate to provide an additional guarantee for this. The amendment in part No. 26 takes care of this. It states that the minister concerned must give his consent before anyone can be so profiled, so before a complete or almost complete picture can be obtained of aspects of the private life of individuals or organisations. Does the Minister share that view? I'd like a response to that. Responsible data analysis also extends to the presentation of that data. I asked questions about this in the preparatory round. Most of them have been answered. The reliability of the data and a source indication are mentioned, but I did not get a clear answer about the context of information. How is the context of the data transmitted to the researcher looking at the product of the automated analysis? An example. It makes quite a difference for the risk assessment of the AIVD whether I watch jihad movies because I would radicalize, or that I do it because I am a terrorist spokesperson of D66 in the Lower House. I come to my final point, point five: better consideration of cooperation with foreign services. There has been a great deal to be done on this subject in recent years. What are the Americans doing at satellite station Buren? Have Dutch services obtained data through friendly services that they were not allowed to acquire themselves? How seriously is it actually considered how a foreign service respects its own legislation and its own fundamental rights? How do we share data with neighbouring countries as effectively as possible to stop terrorists? In order to develop two D66-motions of former colleague and former D66-spokesman Gerard Schouw in this field, the CTIVD has conducted research into the implementation of the cooperation criteria by the AIVD and the STUDY, which was report No. 48.... and the exchange of unevaluated data by AIVD and STIVD, and that was Report No. 49. I have a few questions and an amendment on both subjects. In order to weigh responsibly whether a foreign service is eligible for cooperation, AIVD and STIVD have to make a weighting note. That note must show to what extent the foreign service in question meets the legal criteria for cooperation, the legal powers and technical possibilities of that service, the risks involved in cooperation and the conditions under which such risks can be mitigated as much as possible. Those notes are therefore an important guarantee, both to prevent information falling into wrong, potentially dangerous hands, and to protect privacy. Only now it appears that the AIVD and the STIVD gave little priority to these notes until the beginning of 2015. Although a catch-up is now underway, the CTIVD reported in May 2016 that the concepts are limited in depth and quantity, and that information on parts is missing. That worries me. What has happened since then, I ask the rulers. How many notes are ready now? What part of the countries with whose services we cooperate systematically or occasionally are those notes? As a result of the delay, the legislative proposal contains a special piece of transitional law. Article 166 of the bill states that the weighting notes are only required two years after the rest of the law. Two years. Until then, cooperation relations can apparently be entered into without consideration and without ministerial consent. How do the ministers view that? How are they going to fulfil their ministerial responsibility in this area for two years? Why don't we just delete this transitional article? That is what I propose in my amendment to item No. 50. Why don't we just say to the services: those notes should have been ready by now; make sure there's a workable version? Over the last few years, intelligence and security services have repeatedly demonstrated the need to cooperate across national borders. Terrorists travel easily from place to place in the European Union. When terrorists spread their preparations across several countries or choose the country with the weakest service, they remain easily out of sight. So sharing information is necessary and fortunately this is happening more and more and better. The bill also offers plenty of room for that. That goes so far that, without a cooperation relationship or a weighting note, unevaluated data may be provided without the Minister's consent. That means a dense box of information can be given to an intelligence agency we don't know. It makes me uncomfortable. Of course, the law states that this can only be done on grounds of urgency and after the Minister's consent, but how can the Minister make a responsible assessment if he or she does not know what information he or she is handing over and who he or she is handing over that information to? Another guarantee on this point is of little use: informing the NCTV immediately can only mean something for the future. It cannot, with retroactive effect, reverse the knowledge of unevaluated data by a foreign service. My amendment, which has already been mentioned, to point No. 50 also deletes sharing unevaluated data with these unknown services. I'd like a response. Then I also have a question about sharing raw data with a service that we do work with, for example the NSA, the CIA or the GCHQ. How do we know that the unevaluated tap doesn't contain any confidential traffic from a lawyer or journalist? How do we know that the Patriot Act does not remove the protection that would be required in the Netherlands as regards the protection of these data? I would like to comment on this important point. In doing so, I have completed my five main themes and come to the discussion of my amendments which were not to be included in those five main themes. This is how things go: you opt for a build-up, but a number of amendments have not been discussed. The first amendment concerns the protection of the source of a journalist. The Netherlands has already been convicted several times for lack of source protection. This bill does not seem to provide sufficient protection. The NCTV has concerns about this point and in November Free Press Unlimited, the Dutch Association for Journalists and the Society of Redacteurs also expressed strong criticism of the new bill we are discussing today. That is why I have the amendment to item No. Forty-nine. This seeks to repair this in two respects, firstly by making the provisions of Article 27 of the draft law for the protection of communication between lawyer and client also applicable to data relating to the identity of a source of a journalist obtained through the exercise of a special competence, and secondly by adjusting the definition of what is a source of a journalist. It now reads, according to the bill: persons who have provided information to a journalist for disclosure. Why, my group wonders, should such information have been provided for disclosure? How can the AIVD or STUDY find out for what purpose these data have been provided to the journalist? Why not simply follow the definition of the European Court of Human Rights, which reads: any person who provides information to a journalist? The amendment therefore deletes "for disclosure" from the definition. This also protects sources that provide background information and want to remain off the record, or which are otherwise not directly linked to a publication.

ParlaMint-NL_2017-02-08-tweedekamer-10.u12	The amendment, of course, concerns the combination of'systematic' and 'large scale'. We think that's too much like a dragnet, and we don't want that.
