We make self-driving cars that connect with each other via sensors on the road. We build smart energy meters that charge the electric car, spin the laundry at night or make coffee in the morning. The baby cradle cradles when the baby weeps and the baby bump keeps track of how the baby breathes and sleeps and sounds the alarm if necessary. Or think of the sleep, heart rate or step meter you're wearing or Amazon digital butler Alexa. It works on your voice and seeks information for you or orders you an Uber. Another example is Sony's contact lens camera, a contact lens that can always film as long as you have your eyes open. These are new digital techniques that offer unprecedented opportunities to make our lives easier and solve problems, but they are also technologies that offer unprecedented new possibilities for eavesdropping, espionage or hacking: the car, for example, when you're on the move or your phone or laptop when you're at home. Secret services that watch and read with you when you're e-mailing or dating, when you're calling your loved one or when you're doing banking. Even if you don't work with that computer or phone, these devices can be used by others as a camera or microphone. In the future it can also be done via chips in the body. It was not so long ago, 2002, that the Chamber passed the Intelligence and Security Services Act. Since then, the world has changed, much changed. We have finally put the 20th century behind us, the time of power blocks and the struggle between nation states. We live in a world where great dangers arise from informal networks operating internationally, with infinite opportunities to sow fear and disrupt our society. We must fight this and our secret services must be able to warn us about it. The SP agrees with both ministers that a new law on the secret services is needed, but we are far from convinced that this is the right law. We're not alone in this. The Council of State has a lot of criticism, as well as the CTIVD supervisor, the Justice Council and the Human Rights College. We have three principles in our assessment of this law. The action of secret services must be proportionate, first of all. This means that especially potential perpetrators, such as potential terrorists, should be the subject of investigation and innocent citizens as little as possible. The law must also clarify what we approve of the AIVD and the STIVD, also with a view to technological developments in the future. We need to know what we will say yes to now if we adopt this law. Finally, of course, the law must also be effective. He must be able to protect our citizens as effectively as possible from terrorism, espionage, fraud and abuse. We still have a lot of questions. We have many doubts as to whether this law is proportionate, clear and effective. But, of course, it begins with us taking our secret services seriously, believing the information that our services provide and investing in sufficient people and resources. In 2003, the Netherlands went to war against Iraq. That was on the initiative of the United States, which had warned at the United Nations that Iraq had weapons of mass destruction and was about to use them. The AIVD and the STIVD had no confirmation of the claims of the U.S. secret services and even questioned their claims. But our government decided not to believe our own secret services, but America's. We know the result. Iraq was shot in the head and ISIS grabbed her chance. This terrorist movement managed to create a caliphate in which the citizens are brutally oppressed and to commit attacks, including in our neighbouring countries. I would like to begin by thanking the employees of the AIVD and the STIVD for the important and difficult work they are doing. If they make mistakes, this is widely measured, not least by myself, because at that time the safety of our citizens is at risk. But if they succeed, they will not receive compliments, because their work and thus their successes remain secret. That is why I would like to congratulate those people today. I would like to say to them: I do not know exactly what you have done, but I do know that you have done a lot of good. It is not for nothing that I have worked so hard to reverse the government's cuts in AIVD and STUDY and given all the support to enable new and much-needed investments to be made. We need the secret services very much, especially in the fight against terrorism. On 15 September 2010, the House of Representatives had a very interesting meeting with Ernst Hirsch Ballin, the then Minister of the Interior, of the CDA-huis. That was almost three years before Edward Snowden's revelations. During that consultation, I asked about our government's views on the very large-scale collection and analysis of information. I also asked whether the government considered this to be an effective means of combating terrorism. "I agree with Mr Van Raak," said Hirsch Ballin. He also said: "The unfocused exploitation of huge data files makes no sense." I ask these ministers why they have turned on this point. Why have they tabled a bill that does allow the unfocused exploitation of huge data files? In recent years, Western Europe has also suffered terrible attacks, in Brussels, Paris, Berlin, London, Madrid, Copenhagen and elsewhere. Each time we saw that the attackers were known to the secret services, but that information about them was not exchanged well enough and not fast enough. We also saw that assailants were part of international networks that were mostly in the picture, but that were not dealt with enough. I asked the minister earlier about an American government investigation from May 2015 which indicates that mass retrieval of bulk data would not have contributed to preventing attacks. However, the Minister did not answer that in the written answers, although this question is, of course, of the utmost importance in order to assess whether the extension of powers is really necessary. Ministers want the secret services to be able to "create research-oriented interception in the cable domain." That means getting unfocused information in bulk. In ordinary human language means that the AIVD and the STIVD can go along with everything that happens on the internet. This law is as it is called "technology independently formulated." This means that the law deliberately does not include all the technologies that the secret services are allowed to break into. I agree with the ministers that this is very useful, because we do not know which technologies will be developed in the future and because we do not have to change the law every time. But I ask the Ministers whether they also see the disadvantage of opening the door to the services to work on new technologies, some of which I mentioned at the beginning. We cannot even imagine these new technologies. The Ministers are actually asking us to agree to a law that will enable the secret services to work on new future technologies, which we do not yet know. I don't think you should ask that question to the House of Representatives. In any case, I think that we are following the technology far too much in politics. Scientists and companies are developing technologies. We are then asked what we think about it and whether we can regulate it, and I think it is much more important for politics to discuss which technologies we like and which we do not. Ministers say that greater powers for the secret services can be justified by the extension of supervision. For example, ministers need to authorise the use of funds more often than now. I think these ministers know very well what is going on, but of course they are not independent. That's why there's going to be a Review Committee on Bet Powers, the TIB, consisting of three people. They're independent, but I don't think they know what's going on. The Council of State, the CTIVD Court of Justice and the CTIVD Supervisory Board also have many doubts about this review committee, as it does not receive the information necessary for supervision. And what happens if this review committee approves a performance, but the CTIVD supervisor has to reject it later? Ministers want a technology-independent law, so that we do not have to adapt the law to every new technology, but the human infringement is not technologically independent. It matters whether the services are watching via your smartphone or taking over the control of your electric car. The Chamber must be able to take part in the decision on the use of new technologies, even in the future. Now the CTIVD supervisor can give us access to her reports on the use of new techniques, as recently happened with the use of DNA. That is not structural, however, and that was only afterwards. I therefore ask the Ministers how we in the House of Representatives can in future be informed of the use of new powers through new technologies, so that the Chamber can discuss this and form an opinion on it before this kind of new powers are actually used. An increasingly important area of competence is hacking, including through third parties and also through innocent citizens. This can be very targeted at suspects and in all technologies connected to the Internet. That may be a very good tool for secret services, especially in the fight against potential terrorists, but the hack of one is also a gateway for the other. The AIVD and STIVD use weaknesses in systems, but I ask the ministers whether we should not correctly report and close these weaknesses. Companies may also be required to cooperate in decrypting information. I believe that citizens and businesses should be as secure as possible, for example through encryption. What is the difference between undoing an encryption and weakening encryption? When information is stored massively, in bulk, it can only be processed automatically. That will happen with models and algorithms that create patterns and profiles. The ministers themselves say that these analyses give correlations and not causality. I quote from the ministers' documents: 'A correlation should always be considered, as findings may otherwise lead to stigmatisation and lead to discrimination'. Can the Ministers explain how we are going to do that? Are the AIVD and the STUDY ready for this? I'm afraid not. Monitoring automated processes can, I fear, also only be automated. It is a kind of meta-supervisory by CTIVD supervisor on the processes, but not on the consequences for individuals. Of course, even greater is the problem if our services are to share unevaluated data with services from other countries. We do not know what kind of data is being shared, we do not know what kind of operations are being carried out and we do not know what kind of measures are being taken on the basis of them. However, this is done with data that our services have collected about our own citizens. This material can then be shared with our services by other services, which can also be used again. All this is done on the basis of information that the AIVD and the STUDY are not supposed to have under this law. Does the Minister see a big hole in the law here? We held an interesting debate with these ministers on cooperation with foreign intelligence services in April 2014. Edward Snowden's revelations were the direct cause for this. Looking back on this, I have to say that this has been an uncomfortable debate because ministers have always talked about protecting our own citizens from our own services, while, of course, the great danger to our citizens comes from the action of foreign services. Our services are now making a better assessment of cooperation, but I share the concerns of CTIVD supervisor that this is mainly on paper. I also don't understand why the application was delayed by two years. When countries abuse our common fight against terrorism, we must appeal to countries politically. It is scandalous that we continue to leave whistleblower Snowden to his fate and not help him, for example by granting him political asylum in our country. Ministers held a public consultation for a first version of this law, which led to an unusually high level of criticism and unprecedented critical comments. On this basis, too, the Minister has made a new plan and we are discussing it in this Chamber today. Moreover, this treatment has led to a number of adjustments. Despite all this discussion and despite all these changes, the criticism remains, not least of supervisor CTIVD. I would therefore ask the Ministers to respond to all this criticism, including the latest criticism that the CTIVD sent us after the changes made by the Minister. In particular, I would like to know whether they agree with the CTIVD that it is currently unable to carry out a proper review and audit because there are no concrete legal safeguards to be assessed. I would like the ministers to go into all the points mentioned by the CTIVD. I would like to mention concerns about the implementation of the duty of care, especially in the case of automated data processing, concerns about the targeted and selective use of powers, concerns about the effectiveness of supervision, the use of hacking powers and the protection of our citizens in the sharing of information with foreign services. I would also like them to respond to the obligation to keep data for three years, an unnecessarily long period which is much longer than in the neighbouring countries. I would also like to hear what the ruling of the European Court of Justice on the retention obligation means for this law. The discussion of this law focuses mainly on the collection, processing and processing of digital information, but this law is of course about much more, namely about everything that the AIVD and the STUDY can and can do. Earlier, I mentioned the collection of DNA. In this connection, I asked the Minister what he meant by the return of material. The minister didn't want to answer that earlier. That makes me very curious. I would therefore like to hear a more detailed explanation. Who can ask the secret services to search people? How is this governed by this law? Of course, we know the example of Prince Bernard, who in the past had his friends at the service asking about people. Is that out of the question? In order to defend our freedom and the rule of law, several institutions in our country have a different role. Secret services should be able to operate secretly against persons and organisations operating underground. The task of journalists is precisely to get information on the table and in public. Both these tasks are important to our rule of law. Those two tasks should not be confused. The new law also allows secret services to contact anyone to become a cop or to provide information, including journalists. I think it's sanding. That is why I have tabled an amendment to that effect. This also applies to journalists abroad, especially in war zones. If the journalist is discovered, all other journalists are also at risk. The same applies to emergency workers. I made the proposal to exclude journalists from people who may be approached by the secret services. This also applies to aid workers abroad. It is good that the Minister has laid down detailed rules for eavesdropping journalists and lawyers, including with a view to protecting sources. In that case, permission from the judge is required. I would like to extend that to other groups, such as notaries and representatives of the people. I was also wondering how that actually works for politicians like MPs. For our work, it is very important for us to be able to gather information freely, including reports from whistleblowers, for example. People should then be able to trust that they can speak confidentially to members of parliament, for example. Members of parliament must be sure that their sources are always well protected. This is also one of the various tasks we have. These are also different roles that we have in our rule of law. Can the Minister tell us exactly how the process of eavesdropping politicians is going, who decides about it, who is aware of it and how we can defend ourselves against it? The current Secret Service Act states that people who have been subject to investigation will receive a notice after five years: the obligation to notify. The AIVD didn't want to stick to this at first. We therefore had to move heaven and earth to make sure that the service actually did so. I even had to promise a real cake when the first citizens got a note. Eventually, the AIVD fulfils this duty; with a lot of people's commitment. The STUDY doesn't comply with this law yet. I am glad that the notification obligation has remained, but it must be more than a paper construction, a paper duty. How are we going to ensure that many more notifications are sent to citizens? If people have complaints, they can contact the CTIVD supervisor. There is much to say for that, because the CTIVD is best able to obtain and assess information. I still wonder why the role of the National Ombudsman has fallen completely. Any citizen who wishes to report a wrongdoing can go to the House for whistleblowers, which can also allow an independent investigation. An exception has been made for the secret services. How is the co-operation between the house and the CTIVD? Is the CTIVD willing and competent to investigate at the request of the house? Can whistleblowers involved in secret services have an investigation conducted by the House for whistleblowers? Do whistleblowers who cannot now go to the House but who do file a complaint with the CTIVD always get legal protection? The Minister has taken time for this law. He also listened to criticism. But still, this law isn't right. I have to assess whether the law is proportionate. Then I have to say no right now. For example, because we still have a lot of questions about the need to collect bulk data. I have to judge whether this law is clear, and I have to say no at the moment. If only because we give our secret services a free pass to work on technologies that we can hardly imagine today, but which will still intervene deeply in people's lives. But there is also another question about what this law actually has to do with reality, whether it really contributes to the protection of our citizens. This law deals with the rules we lay down for our secret services for dealing with our citizens. But no matter how critical I am about this, it does not, of course, contain the real danger. That comes from Russia, China, Iran, Israel or the United States, where the new President Donald Trump will be very little interested in what law we approve here in this Parliament. In the world of secret services there are no laws and regulations, but power and counterpower. This applies to our old enemies, but unfortunately also to our supposed friends. In April 2014, we had a debate about espionage on our citizens, also by friendly countries. Since then, the government has been deafeningly silent. Today we are talking about a law, but we must also talk about a new reality, about how our secret services, but above all those of other, friendly countries, treat our citizens decently. I am very interested in the response of these ministers.
ParlaMint-NL_2017-02-08-tweedekamer-10.u21	Mr. Chairman. Security and privacy, two fundamental fundamental rights that every citizen needs to be able to live freely. Two fundamental fundamental rights which, unfortunately, also often bite each other, as well as the present Intelligence and Security Services Act. The government wants to give the General Intelligence and Security Service and the Military Intelligence and Security Service the powers to rake and store large amounts of data through a towing network. For our safety, of course, but what does that do with our privacy? Is the law careful enough? Do we even see all the consequences of a dragnet? What data of Dutch citizens will end up with security services from other countries via our own AIVD and STIVD? Will Donald Trump's NSA be able to read with our whatsapples later? Those are all questions that many people live with. Questions to which I cannot yet answer the electorate, even after the very comprehensive answer given by the cabinet. Leave it at the forefront: GroenLinks wants a safe Netherlands that is protected from terrorists 24 hours a day seven days a week as much as possible. The Netherlands has so far been able to avoid the series of bloody attacks in Europe, partly thanks to the work of our security services. They risk their own safety for the safety of all of us. A sacrifice that we probably don't appreciate enough, because the work of the services is hidden behind closed doors. At the same time, we cannot defend our rule of law by abolishing it, because we need that rule of law more than ever. There is also an inherent risk of mass storage of data on citizens. Where one government can be expected to handle these sensitive data in a decent and proportionate manner, a completely different regime can be in power the next day. Safety does not necessarily benefit from mass surveillance. Our security services must have sufficient resources, stability and capable people. This will help us to be safe. This cabinet has made a mess of it over the past period by always fluctuating in the money spent on the services. Fortunately, the rest now seems to have been somewhat restored. The peace and the means for the AIVD and the STIVD to do their job is what we have to offer from the political point of view. One of the reasons for a new law is that the Minister wants to make the powers of the departments as independent as possible. And there is something to be said for, of course, the technology of 2002, the year of the current law, is not comparable to today's technology. At the same time, we must guard against unintended effects. The emergence of the Internet or things increases the possibilities of tracking people via espionage on devices. The story that the law creates the possibility to spy through chips in the human body is, of course, nonsense, but it seems to GroenLinks or advisable to give a clearer definition of which equipment the services may hack. The law now states that this can be done on all automated works, but that's a blank check. As far as GroenLinks is concerned, the principle of legal certainty requires further colouring. People have the right to know which devices may be hacked by the security services. If we regulate this interpretation of General Measure of Governance, the definition can then easily change over time. In his written reply, the Minister stated that he did not see any reason to do so. I would like to hear a more comprehensive response from the Minister. Why doesn't he see a reason for that? Then there is the most important change that the law brings: the power of towing nets. During his answer in the written round of questions, the Minister replied that GroenLinks uses the term "dragnet power." The minister prefers to use his own term: research assignment-oriented interception. That's a term that hangs on the edge of truth. It is precisely unfocused interception that is at stake. It's about interception of communication between people without looking at who it's about. Security services have to draw up an investigative order before they are allowed to throw out a towing net. A request for a note to the Minister asking whether they can tap all communication at busy stations in the Netherlands for a year: to call that targeted interception, is flirting with deception. What a research question for a towing net has to meet, is not clear from the law, and is therefore not testable. Is it true that such a research question can actually cover everything? If that is not the case, can the Minister give us some examples of research questions that could not be? Is he then prepared to include this restriction in the text of the law? Data retrieved may be stored for no less than three years. The Council of State advised us to make that a year. That would be better for the relationship with the privacy of citizens. The cabinet doesn't want to do that. I have therefore tabled an amendment to settle that. On the question of who is to pay for the expensive storage of all these data, the Minister gives a rather vague answer that is more or less: we will see that again. Any financial coverage for this is lacking. A study by the United States' Privacy and Civil Liberties Oversight Board in 2014 shows that bulk interception in the United States has made a concrete difference in combating terrorism in no less than zero cases. Anyone who sells that factual analysis against the potentially very serious breach of privacy that makes a towing net power can only conclude that we should not want to do so. GroenLinks is therefore opposed to creating a dragnet power, not because we categorically always place privacy above security, but because the effectiveness of bulk interception has simply not been proven. It provides false security and bogus privacy. I have therefore tabled an amendment, which was co-signed by Mr Van Raak, to delete the power of towing net. I can imagine that the Minister will not be overwhelmed by enthusiasm for this amendment, but it is up to the Chamber to make a clear choice through the vote on this amendment whether it is for or against towing power. Let me turn to the issue of unread data abroad. The data collected by the AIVD and the STIVD may then be transmitted unread to foreign security services. Unread! So we don't know what we're sending. The law thus allows us, for example, to send data on Dutch people with an Eritrean background to Eritrea's security service. Of course, the Minister says we're not going to do that. Services will only transmit unread data in exceptional situations, for example if there is an imminent attack. The question then is: why can't these data be read just as quickly before they are forwarded? Does the cabinet want the services to forward so much unread data that it cannot look at what is in it? And, if so, why on earth? If that is not the case, why should there be a possibility in the law? GroenLinks believes that the infringement involved, not only on privacy but possibly even on our national interests, does not outweigh the ill-disputed benefits. I therefore have the amendment to item No. 46 to ensure that it is no longer possible to transmit unread data to foreign security services. In addition, I have the amendment to item No. 22, which makes this impossible for security services of countries with which our services do not cooperate, in case the amendment in part No. 46 the Court has not received sufficient support. We should not end up in the situation where our security services become relay doors for, for example, the US NSA or the Russian FSB. We must certainly not end up in the situation where our security services are passing on to the security services in Eritrea. From the Minister's reply, I would point out that the government also considers it undesirable that this should happen. I hope for a positive response to these amendments. The law paves the way for its own DNA database for the services. The cabinet states that it responds to the criticism of the Supervisory Committee of the Intelligence and Security Services. The criticism of the CTIVD was that the storage of DNA material by the AIVD was unlawful. In doing so, of course, the CTIVD did not say that it would be in favour of making it lawful in this new law. It is a political choice to give the security services their own DNA database. GroenLinks thinks that choice goes too far. In our view, the government has not sufficiently argued why this is necessary. At present, the services also have to do without their own DNA database and use the expertise and services of the Dutch Forensic Institute and certified laboratories. Why wouldn't that be enough? GroenLinks does not consider the own DNA database for the security services to be demonstrably necessary. The database would lead to an unjustified breach of citizens' privacy. I have therefore tabled an amendment to delete it, namely the Amendment No 2. 44. I'm here to talk about encryption. The law introduces a duty for companies and experts to cooperate in the decryption of encryption. That is in itself something you can question. Do you have to cross the principled border to force people to become co-responsible for the actions of security services? That's not even the most important question we need to answer. We absolutely must avoid putting pressure on manufacturers and developers to build vulnerabilities in favour of security services. Vulnerabilities can be used not only by security services but also by malicious hackers. GroenLinks therefore wants the legislative text to be crystal clear. I have the amendment to item No. 25 submitted to clarify this. As the Minister stated in his written reply that it is not the government's intention to make this possible, I assume that that amendment can count on a positive opinion. I'll come to the review. Especially when the AIVD and STUDY are allowed to use a coarse instrument such as towing power, a rigorous and independent control of the use of such powers is crucial. GroenLinks is therefore pleased that the Review Committee on Bet Powers is being set up. It is a good thing to check in advance whether the use of the power is lawful and that the Minister must comply in his opinion. We do wonder why the government has not chosen to have these tests carried out by a special court. When it comes to taps with lawyers, the court in The Hague is also called in. I would like to hear from the Minister what the objections would be to such a model. GroenLinks is less enthusiastic about the ex post review. We would like the CTIVD to be able to make a binding assessment of the legality of the implementation of powers. If the CTIVD finds that, for example, dragnet data or DNA profiles are stored for too long, it must be able to stop the power immediately. This is what the CTIVD itself is asking for in its response to this law. It argues that the law does not strengthen the CTIVD sufficiently. It is also referring to the non-binding nature of the committee's opinion. The Minister argues against this by stating that the opinion of the CTIVD is also not binding under the current law. That's right, but the current law doesn't have any power of towing. More powers with a greater breach of the privacy of Dutch people require that they be monitored more closely. That is why I have also tabled an amendment on this point, namely the Amendment No 2. 43. GroenLinks wants the CTIVD, when it finds an unlawful act, to be able to order the cessation of the exercise of a power until it has been reported to the Chamber. As a result, the Chamber can control the Minister's handling of the CTIVD's judgment. If the Minister ignores this judgment, the Chamber may summon the Minister to reconsider that decision. This is how we organise democratic control of the services. I hope that the Minister will appreciate the importance of this and look favourably at the amendment. GroenLinks is pleased that the minister seems to have listened carefully to the Chamber when it comes to eavesdropping the communication between lawyers and their clients. That's what the judge has to judge now. However, the protection of the sources of journalists, which should be at the same level as regards GroenLinks, is not complete. There must be as many safeguards as possible to prevent a malicious government from eavesdropping communication between journalists and sources through intelligence services. That would affect the basis of our rule of law. I therefore hope that the Minister will respond positively to the amendment on this subject. The new Act on Intelligence and Security Services, if adopted, will mark a new era in the work of the security services. GroenLinks is not against modernising the law. We understand our country's interest in security services that operate effectively, but this needs to be done safely, carefully and with respect for people's privacy. There must also be good democratic control. The legislative proposal as it stands cannot therefore count on the support of GroenLinks. That is why I have put forward many proposals to make the law safer and more careful. GroenLinks can only agree to this law if people's protection and privacy are significantly improved. We hear people say, "Well, as long as you have nothing to hide, you don't have to fear anything." That's a big misconception. We all have something to hide: our medical information, our deepest fears and desires, the question of who we hate, and sometimes who we love. Who always has the curtains open in his house? Who goes to the bathroom without locking the door? Who doesn't have a password on his computer or phone? If we have nothing to hide, why doesn't everyone in the house have a 24-hour camera with a live feed to Facebook? Everyone has something to hide. That makes us human and gives us personality. This makes privacy as fundamental as security. Our society cannot live without one or the other. That is why I hope that we will make the legislative proposal before us better and more careful, that we will not allow privacy and security to bite each other, but that we will put together a strong legislative proposal that will properly define the frameworks for the security services for the future.
ParlaMint-NL_2017-02-08-tweedekamer-10.u22	Mr. Chairman. I start by saying once more that we are living in a digital revolution. Big developments in data change our lives in many ways. And revolutionary times are always turbulent times. We see and feel that the world is changing around us and we don't yet know where it's going to end. Revolutionary times give opportunities for major improvements, but also give threats to the security and prosperity of the Netherlands. These are known and unprecedented threats. The threats we know are already not wrong. Think of terrorism, manipulating our elections, stealing our expensive innovations, shutting down the economy, opening locks and driving, and so on. Those are all digital threats. The task of our intelligence and security services is to identify those known and unprecedented threats and thus protect us. And so we cannot be naive and must allow our services to continue their work in this data revolution. For our safety, we need to bring the services back to time so that they can handle these revolutionary exciting times in which we live now when it comes to our security. Moreover, the law lays down, in particular, current practice in strict and verifiable rules. Since 2002, the year of that old law, a great deal has changed in the way the services operate. Those changes have now been codified. That's good. But to leave it at that, or even to restrict current practice, as some parties seem to want, is at best naive and only hypocritical and dangerous. The image that you would only be well-equipped to digital threats by old means, denies reality. That only searching for the person would be more effective than using the technique to see at all what is going on is a misconception. That is putting your head in the sand and then screaming murder and fire again at the next terrorist debate about a minister who has his or her affairs not in order, or because information has not been shared. You can't run a country like that.

ParlaMint-NL_2017-02-08-tweedekamer-10.u99	Mr. Chairman. We're discussing the new Intelligence and Security Services Act today. In these services, a lot of people are working every day to keep our country safe, and I would like to thank them for that first. They represent an important line of defence in the fight against terrorism and other threats to our national security. It is therefore also important that the services have adequate opportunities to do their job. In 2013, the Dessens Commission launched a new law on intelligence and security services. Social and technological developments have made the 2002 law obsolete. That calls for a renewal of the law. There is a need for a technology-neutral formulation. The proposal before us seeks to achieve this. That has become a very thick suit, with all kinds of safeguards and other powers. The new bill has been critically received in parts, not only by privacy organisations, but also by the CTIVD, for example; this has just been mentioned. The CTIVD already supervises the work of the services afterwards. My group is therefore also looking at parts of this legislative proposal with a critical eye. At the same time, my group realises that it is in the interests of our security to give our services powers that allow them to continue to do their important work in an adequate manner. We must therefore seek the classic balance between security and privacy, between the right to a personal and inalienable domain where the government must remain outside, and the special responsibility of the government to act in the public interest and the interest of national security. Two important principles apply to the Christian Union in this law. Firstly, there is the question of the need for the new powers. The cabinet formulates new, far-reaching powers for the services. We would like it to be well-founded as to why these powers are needed. We must know whether these powers are necessary. Secondly, the adage: no powers without proper safeguards. That is important, in order to be able to embezzle those powers properly. I have the necessary questions on both points. I shall begin with the need for the new powers. As has been said, the government must be able to make it sufficiently clear that it is necessary to make a further, more serious breach of citizens' privacy in order to serve all our interests. It's complicated that we don't know what we don't know. The government cannot share everything about the work of the services, their limitation and the risks involved. That makes Parliament, in a sense, wandering around in the dark. ParlaMint-NL_2017-02-08-tweedekamer-10.u179	Mr. Chairman. The work of the intelligence and security services is indispensable for the protection and defence of the democratic rule of law. The work of the intelligence and security services is indispensable for the safety of the Dutch citizens and for the safety of the Dutch military on mission. For the CDA Group, there is no doubt that the powers of the services need to be modernised. Technological developments in the field of information and communication are accelerating. We are fully reaping the benefits: we are connected to the Internet day and night and we are connected to each other via computer and mobile phone, we are watching Netflix and there is plenty of gaming. However, the growing dependence on the same technology also makes our society vulnerable. That is why we must constantly invest in the means by which we protect and defend our society. The same applies to the police and the judiciary, the same applies to defence, and so does our intelligence and security services. This cabinet started over four years ago with a substantial cut in the AIVD: the removal of the foreign task. As quickly it was clear that this was a reckless austerity measure in a tense world. With the report on cuts and intensifications in the AIVD, the General Court has put the finger on the sore spot. The Court concluded that the government agreement did not take sufficient account of the particular dynamics of a knowledge-intensive organisation such as the AIVD. The CDA Group considered and considers it necessary to restore the IAVD's budget. There was an urgent need to raise financial resources, but there is also an urgent need to strengthen the powers of the services. As early as December 2013, the Dessens Committee concluded that the Intelligence and Security Services Act needed to be amended. It advocated that the services should also be able to operate on the cable when there are threats to national security. Cyber attacks and digital espionage pose a new threat to national security. STUDY and AIVD are allowed to intercept data and telecommunication traffic unfocused within the current law if that traffic goes through the ether or via the satellite. The explosive growth of international cable networks makes it increasingly important for these services to be able to explore and analyse cable-linked Internet traffic in the interests of national security, the Dessens Committee concluded. The same committee stressed the need for a new balance between the effective operation of the intelligence and security services and the legal guarantees. That balance must be firmly anchored in the law. Today we are finally discussing the legal form in which the Dessens Committee's recommendations are formulated. Can the Minister explain exactly how far this bill is going? Part of it concerns the definition of existing powers of the services, part about new powers and part about the definition and extension of existing safeguards. For the CDA Group, the effective functioning of the services and adequate supervision of the services are not available separately. I would remind you of the remarks made by Commission President Dessens when presenting the report: 'Citizens, directors and representatives of the people must be able to be firmly convinced that our intelligence and security services work within a legal framework. A framework that gives sufficient recognition that their particular position is only acceptable if those services are embedded in a confidence-building system of steering, monitoring and transparency." Does the present bill contain that confidence-building legal framework? That is the question we need to answer today. Does the bill meet the requirements of the European Convention on Human Rights? In other words, is the Strasbourg proof bill? That's what I'm asking the Minister. At first sight, it is a legal and technical question, but above all it is a question of the adequacy of the resources of the services. These are means by which the services protect national security, the security of civilians and the security of military personnel on missions. That is a core task of our government. Then there's supervision. At the request of the CTIVD, the Department of State and Administrative Law of Leiden University conducted research into the human rights framework for the Dutch system of supervision of the intelligence and security services in 2015. The researchers concluded that the case law of the European Court of Human Rights showed a strong preference for prior authorisation by an independent authority for the use of collusive investigative powers that deeply interfere with the privacy of our citizens. In the legislative proposal, this review is entrusted in advance to the Review Committee on Bet Powers, the TIB. The legality judgment of the TIB is binding, but at the same time the powers and possibilities of the TIB are limited. The TIB will assess the same information the Minister has. Unlike the CTIVD, the TIB does not have the powers and possibilities to request the underlying information from the services. Can the Minister explain how the TIB's prior assessment will relate to the CTIVD's research possibilities? How is it ensured that the TIB has sufficient expertise, knowledge and capacity, so that ex ante verification is not a formality, but leads to a sound judgment? The Leiden researchers further concluded that ECHR case-law results from the requirement of binding ex post legality supervision by an external independent supervisor. Can the Minister tell us how this binding legality oversight was implemented in the legislative proposal? Finally, the researchers also conclude that ECHR case-law lays down an imperative requirement that independent supervision may make a binding assessment in complaint procedures. That's now invested in the CTIVD. In the legislative proposal, that binding judgment in complaint procedures is entrusted to the CTIVD. Can the Minister explain how the various roles of this CTIVD will relate to each other? There is also discussion as to whether the bill is "Strasbourg proof" in terms of the three-year retention period for data collected in the framework of research assignment-oriented interception. Can the Minister explain again why a three-year retention period is proportionate? For the CDA Group, the demand for the length of the retention period is not crucial, but whether sufficient guarantees are provided that the data for which they were collected are used. In the report, the CDA Group raised the question of how the government guarantees that the storage of data on servers or in the cloud is always under Dutch law. If data from those services are on servers on Dutch territory, there is no problem. But does that also apply if data from those services are stored in the cloud? The CDA Group wants to prevent data from being outside the scope of Dutch law by storing the services on servers outside Dutch territory, or in the cloud, so that the legal protection of citizens is at stake. Does the Minister recognise that this could be a problem? In what way is it ensured that the data of the services are always covered by Dutch law? The CDA Group believes that it is necessary to extend the powers of the services, as well as to strengthen the supervision of services. It goes without saying that the services must have sufficient resources to actually provide additional guarantees. Supervisors should also have sufficient resources to monitor their operation. They must have sufficient capacity, knowledge and expertise. Can the Minister explain how both services and supervisors are being strengthened? The work of the intelligence and security services is not controlled in the same way as the work of other administrations. The Dessens Committee's evaluation report has produced important recommendations for the effective functioning of the services, as well as for monitoring them. That is why the CDA Group considers the assessment clause in the legislative proposal to be important. Of course, the effectiveness and effects of this law will be evaluated in practice. However, the functioning of the services will also be periodically reviewed. As far as the CDA Group is concerned, this is not a formality, but an essential contribution to the protection and defence of the democratic rule of law.

ParlaMint-NL_2017-02-08-tweedekamer-10.u200	Mr. Chairman. This week, when I was preparing this debate, I found out that I would have gone mad if I had been in a quiz last week and I had been asked when the first security service was set up. I would have dated that somewhere in the nineteenth century. So that's a huge mistake, because the first security service dates back to June 1945: the National Security Office. Four years later, this was followed up by the Homeland Security Service (BVD). In 2002, it was converted into the current General Intelligence and Security Service. I'm not gonna tell you the whole story because I'm gonna leave the military security services out for a while. They date back a little earlier, namely from before the First World War. From the outset, the question of how far the task of this service can or should be accomplished has been discussed. I came across a beautiful quote from Minister Teulings from 1949, when the National Security Office was converted into the Homeland Security Office. I thought it would be nice to present that quote. Teulings was Minister of the Interior, thus the predecessor of the current minister. A quote. The undersigned questions whether a service such as the Homeland Security Service will ever enjoy popularity. Such services are a necessary evil, with emphasis on "needs." As long as powers in the world are engaged in unsavory means to undermine the foundations of our democratic rule of law, the government will need to have a device to identify subversive activities in good time. The undersigned considers that such a device, which by the nature of its activity can never be made public, would be capable of meeting general sympathy. In the meantime, the undersigned would like to pay particular attention to the work of the service so that it remains in line with his mission and objective. He is aware that large groups from all sections of the population are working wholeheartedly with the BVD, convinced if they are convinced of the need to join forces to combat attacks on Christianity and democracy. I could easily imagine that I have now mowed some grass at the feet of the current Minister of the Interior, because when we talk about the AIVD and the STUDY, it is still the same at heart. Such a provision is a necessary evil, with the emphasis on "needs." They're working in secret. This is inevitable and inextricably linked to the nature of the institution. Monitoring the task and objective of the resources deployed is and will continue to be essential. The forces that seek to undermine the rule of law or damage our security are still there. What is also striking to me is that the fight against attacks on Christianity and democracy has become very topical, particularly when we look at where the recent terrorist threat comes from. In 2017, there are indeed no-nonsense forces that are doing their utmost to undermine the stability of the Christian West. As much as you can discuss the Christian content of our society, it is clear that it is rooted in Christianity. Let's cherish and protect that. This civilisation must be protected from the radical and terrorist attacks, especially from the extremist-Islamic angle. Especially after 11 September 2001, this task has only gained in value and intensity. The current law is obsolete. The Dessens Committee rightly pointed out in 2013 that technological developments call for new powers. Anyone who goes through the gloomy reactions to this bill almost gets the impression that if this bill goes through, the Netherlands will become a sort of police state of North Korean allure. But if you let the bill get through to you well, you will see that protecting public security and protecting citizens' private lives and privacy go hand in hand. The extent to which privacy is infringed will increase as the threat of public security is increased. The SGP does not believe in an all-controlling state, but it does not believe in the absolute inviolability of individual privacy. As far as we are concerned, there are three important principles that play a role in the assessment of this legislative proposal. Firstly, the purpose of intelligence must be clear: security, the protection of the democratic legal order or other interests of the State or the community. Secondly, a slightly different application of the concept of proportionality. The least extreme agent should be used. It must also be proportionate to the intended result. Thirdly, the recognition that far-reaching infringements are sometimes necessary and acceptable, as long as sufficient counterpower and adequate supervision are organised. The SGP has the impression that this bill provides a good, updated basis for the intelligence work of the coming period, on the basis of what I have just said, a good balance between public security on the one hand and citizens' privacy on the other. The question of whether you are for privacy or security is far too simple. One declares privacy sacred as long as there are no concrete risks. But at the time of an attack, security is declared sacred. Then the immediate question arises as to why the security services did not recognise this danger in good time. Based on a realistic world view, the SGP assumes that man is inclined to all evil. By the way, that is also a very biblical notion. Anyone who realizes that also understands that people are doing everything they can to hide their evils, so that they can now go their own way. This calls for far-reaching powers that have been well and carefully invested. The government, as the SGP confesses on the basis of the Bible, has been given to us to protect us from the evils of attacks and from undermining society. That is why the role of the government in the field of security is very important to us. Necessary, the AIVD and the STIVD are about the use of secret powers. Therefore, proper safeguards are needed to monitor how the use of these powers takes place. That is one of the starting points I have already mentioned. Power always wants more. Power can corrupt very quickly. Society therefore deserves good protection against abuse of power. In doing so, we must be careful not to make the law so powerless that it is actually an impossible horde to be able to protect security. We are talking about unprecedented and unknown threats that could endanger national security. I will conclude with a number of specific questions on the basis of the detailed written documents. The first question concerns the supervisory structure. I ask the Minister to reflect on the following statement. This law has brought the possibilities and powers of the AIVD and the STUDY into line with what the 21st century requires, but the supervisory structure is still based on the possibilities and responsibilities of the 20th century. In other words, does the CTIVD have sufficient resources to carry out checks, whether or not in combination with the already mentioned TIB? After all, the law stands or falls with proper supervision to prevent abuse by the security services. The second question concerns steering. Other stakeholders, in addition to the CTIVD, include the coordinator and the United Intelligence Services Committee Netherlands, the CVIN. The coordinator shall be General Affairs and shall be the chairman of the CVIN. Is the involvement of the most important ministries in this area exactly as envisaged by the Dessens Commission? The third point concerns craftsmanship. The SGP considers it important that the security services are not primarily distrustful, but that there is confidence that they really want to carry out their work in accordance with the law. It is precisely that ex ante review that can only confirm that. However, it must be avoided that this review is carried out in such a way that the speed of action is removed and risk intercepting is made more difficult. What is being done to increase speed so that this monitoring is not at the expense of the quality of the intelligence work? The fourth question concerns access to chat services. The head of the AIVD recently indicated in an interview that it is necessary to break the encryption of chat services such as WhatsApp and Telegram when there are risks. It should be avoided that certain media are specifically suitable for communication between terrorists, because there is no or little access to them. Is the Minister prepared to look again closely at whether the proposal before us offers sufficient possibilities for the break-up of encryption? Isn't under the guise of the importance of encryption not unintentionally facilitating terrorism? My final question concerns the prevention of misuse. After all, it is important that information is lawfully obtained. There has been the necessary discussion about the use of research assignment-oriented interception, also called the dragnet approach. The SGP likes to hear from the minister how the supervisors can monitor in concrete terms that the data that is used as a by-catch of a research assignment is actually destroyed. Is it guaranteed that this information will not actually be used without the Minister's consent and the TIB's approval? And if so, how? The effectiveness of the work of the intelligence and security services must go hand in hand with sufficient legal guarantees and citizens' confidence that the powers that exist are exercised carefully. Our impression is that this proposal strikes a good balance between the right to privacy on the one hand and the right to the protection and security of public space on the other. We look forward to the mayor's response.

ParlaMint-NL_2017-02-08-tweedekamer-10.u226	This is considered a service order from box K. Mr. Chairman. A lot of words have already been exchanged today. That is why I would like to be a little brief, but I would like to emphasise the importance of the work at all. If you consider the recent threats to our security, it's malware, hacks, terrorist attacks or the security of our men and women on a military mission somewhere. The Secretary of Defense will return to that last point. Often the most important and sometimes even only weapon against these threats is that you can stop them by seeing them arrive in advance. If someone is willing to drive a truck into a crowd of people, such as recently in Berlin, you can no longer properly protect people from it when that happens. So the only weapon we have is the so-called combination of intention and potential, so the intention to do something and the ability to do so. The combination of these two is a crucial task in protecting the security of our society. There is a lot of talk in the Chamber about a wide range of public services. The first task in any form of society is, of course, to ensure the safety of the group, of the country, as much as possible. By the way, I believe that everyone shares that. This requires information. As this can often lead to an infringement of private life, the collection of this information can only take place if there is a legal basis for it. That is why it is right and proper that this should be discussed extensively. I am therefore grateful to the Chamber that it has taken up a great deal of space for this in its busy agenda. As I have just said, the new law now before us is in many respects no different from the current law on intelligence and security services. That also says something about the current law. It was conceived and created twenty years ago and finally published in the Official Journal in 2002. That was at a time when mobile telephony, if there was one, was still using large satellite phones. Today everyone has something in their pocket that can do more than the computer we had on our desk 20 years ago. So that law doesn't take into account its potential at all. That is why, at the start of this cabinet period, the Research Committee-Dessens was instructed to evaluate the current law. That committee came up with the advice to eliminate the difference existing in the current law between the collection of information via the ether, via satellite, and the collection of information via cable. Several Members have already said in the first term that this was perhaps a rational difference at the time, but that today every telephone call goes straight into the ground on the first street corner. If you can't access that, you're actually trying to serve safety with one hand behind your back. That was the advice of the Dessens Committee. We discussed this in the Chamber and then we started the legislative process. For the most fundamental point, which was my third point, namely the lifting of technical independence in the bulk collection of information.... is broad support, not only from the Dessens Committee that we set up at that time, but also from the CTIVD, our supervisor, and from the Council of State. When we spoke about this on an earlier occasion, there was also support for this from a rather large majority in the House of Representatives. I close my introduction by saying that you can talk for so long about all details and privacy sometimes it's very important details or even key issues, but in the end nothing is as big an invasion of your privacy as when you go somewhere with your family and don't come back, if you're not safe somewhere. That is what we all see when we deal with this law. I'm moving on to the happy subject of the DNA database. During this period there was a situation in which we did something that was rightly judged unlawful by the supervisory authority. The first time I got the judgment that something was illegal, it scared the shit out of me. I thought, "What the hell?" We're not doing anything illegal, are we? But in itself, this is the way it's supposed to function. We have a law that states in general terms what is allowed and what is not allowed. Then the technique develops. The services seek, to the best of their knowledge, to deal with a new situation within the general terms of the law. And then it happens that a supervisor says: it may be useful and necessary, but it is not something that the law allows, so it is unlawful. Objects were seized in buildings that had previously been occupied by people we knew were involved in jihadist activities. Think about coffee cups and stuff like that. They were kept for the purpose of being able to identify when someone might blow up somewhere. Then you could reconstruct if it was perhaps the one who had been in the safe house before. The basis for this is Article 22 of the current Intelligence and Security Services Act: the service may collect objects for the purpose of establishing identity. There's no mention of DNA testing, because 20 years ago, you were able to identify identities through a physical fingerprint and maybe through hair traces. The supervisor said, of course, it's the intention to derive DNA traces from it, so we can put it under the scope of Article 22. However, it may only be for the purpose of establishing the identity and you must do so. Only save for future situations that may or may not arise, there is no legal basis for this. The supervisor said: you should amend the law. That would be very wise, but then it should include retention periods; only then can we check whether it has been done properly according to the law. However, for this breach of privacy the legal basis was missing and we had to destroy the material. We did. It's been destroyed. Words, of course, have great effects. It was the opening of the newspaper: illegal DNA database of Dutch citizens. This was the situation. We have solved this in the new law by means of an article which says that the material can be stored for a maximum of six months and must be destroyed afterwards. If a DNA profile is derived from it, it may be stored for longer. The object is gone. As practice shows that it can remain useful for a long time, it may be kept for five years. In addition, there are possibilities for extension in the law. This is an example of how it can work well in itself. The supervisor says you've acted with the best of intentions. The intentions are always good; the services are very precise and legal, and try to keep to everything. A new situation had arisen. The services thought: we think this is wise to do. And then it happens so often that the supervisor says: we have looked at it again, but this is still illegal. Then you either have to stop or you have to change the law. Maybe you can come up with something else. So that will continue to happen in the future. It will also be important if I come back to other subjects in a moment. With your permission, I will conclude with the first subject.

ParlaMint-NL_2017-02-08-tweedekamer-10.u230	The answer is yes. Of course, there's no other way. What if there were people in a house in The Hague? They're out of there. There's reason to think they're dangerous people who have terrorist intentions. If they occur again somewhere in the world, it may be useful to know if the DNA trace we have here is the same as the DNA trace of the data subjects elsewhere. That is useful and necessary. The second subject concerns two situations which deserve additional protection and are therefore separate in the law. The first is the interaction between lawyers and clients. The fact that privacy is extra guaranteed is important for a proper course of justice. The second is protecting the source by journalists. This should ensure that, in the interests of freedom of the press, not only can the service, with the agreement of the minister, seek to find a source through a journalist. Both subjects have a long history. The latter has a painful past, because many years ago journalists were held hostage by the government because they refused to reveal their source. The European Court of Justice has said of both situations that it is not absolutely impossible to infringe the legal-client interactions, or that special means are being used to identify the source of a journalist. However, it is such a serious step that this can only be done if the Hague court gives its prior consent separately. So apart from all the other things, at the TIB, that we're going to talk about later, for those two categories an exception has been made in the current law. In the first period, the question was asked as to why these two situations are not dealt with in the same way. The answer is actually in the motivation I just gave. The situation is not the same. In the case of the attorney-client interactions, it is the interaction itself that deserves additional protection. That's just the secrecy of legal protection that a person can get in a state of law. In the interaction between a journalist and a source, what deserves additional protection is that that journalist cannot be used as an entry point to track down terrorists or to find out who his source might have been via a tap on that journalist. The other way around, of course, that doesn't matter. If you have a target in mind because it can't be otherwise, it's subsidiary to other means and it's proportional to tap, then it's just the fact that that target is going to call a journalist, not suddenly a reason to stop the tap. You already had that person in your sights, so you're not trying to find a source. You'll be legitimate in treating a target the way we'd do it with every target. That's why we've incorporated them both into the law, but in a different way. There is a second reason, by the way. That is why both professions are distinguishable, but only the group of lawyers is quite easy to identify. That's just a profession with a fixed list. You can also try filtering it from a large dataset by name or by phone number. For journalists, who have been discussed in the first term, we use the professional definition used by the Dutch Association of Journalists. This is also stated in the paper on the report. I refer to that, because it is a clear definition. However, it is not a exhaustive list and that means that you have to assess on a case-by-case basis whether or not someone is a journalist. So there is also practical reason to have different treatment. Especially with large data sets you can easily filter the lawyers out. But if you have a target in mind that calls at some point with someone who is involved in journalism, you can't just know in advance that it's a journalist. Mr. President, I'm gonna take a breath. I would like to close this second block and move on to the third issue, namely the need to eliminate the technical dependence that now exists in the law when it comes to gathering bulk information. I would add that we are doing nothing but our neighbouring countries, with which we work intensively. In the field of counter-terrorism, for example, the AIVD works together with Germany, France, England and Sweden, all of which make that cable-dependent bulk interception legally possible. Of course, it applies a fortiori to military missions. Allies often go somewhere together from the beginning and want to guarantee the safety of their staff by gathering information. We are already gathering in bulk off the coast of Somalia via the air; the Secretary of Defense can tell us much more about it. That is very useful, because we then map out in advance where potential threats are. There too, however, more and more people are walking around with a mobile phone. On the street corner it goes into the cable and according to the law you are not allowed anymore. That is why it is very important to do so. When we started this process, I asked the AIVD to give me an example of a bulk collection which is not allowed now and which would be useful. Let me give you that example. Let's say we want to identify more Syrians. People go from here to Syria, participate in the jihadist struggle, come back and are, messed up when they are now, willing and perhaps able to make attacks here. For example, you can take a closer look at the traffic between the people here and the people who are active there and collect only metadata for a month: what number in the Netherlands calls with which number in Syria at what time? That's phase one. Ministerial consent must be given to this. You have to justify that it is necessary for national security and that you cannot collect that information in any other way. It must therefore be subsidiary to other means and it must be proportionate. In a moment we will come back to the question of what is proportional. In phase two, for which once again ministerial consent is required, you have all that data and you put it next to a list of known mobile numbers of known Syrians. While you map that, you can see that they are calling each other and calling with a few buddies they know here, but you can also see that the familiar songs are all calling with a song you haven't seen before. That's where phase two stops. We don't know anymore. It could be a facilitator, or someone who acts as a spider in the web of terrorist interactions, but it could also be the pizza baker on the corner. Then we'll move on to phase three. At that stage, we are putting a specific tap on it, in fact traditionally, as it should be under the old law. It must also be necessary and proportionate on the basis of the new law. Then we will see if it is indeed someone who is part of the network. Possibilities to get new targets in view via networks do exist when the communication goes through the ether, but not when it goes through the cable. Similar examples are also worked out in the documents, such as the capture of malware, when it comes to cybercrime, or the mapping of hostile activities in military areas. In the example given, and therefore also in the case of the law as it stands at present, it is not necessary, not useful and not wise to go about collecting just, unfocused. It was quoted in the first term from American research. In that study it was said that the mere collection of information is not right, that you should not want it and that this is not necessary. Reference has also been made to statements made by my predecessor Hirsch Ballin; Mr Van Raak has drawn attention to this before, and I replied to it at that time. The simple collection of information took place shortly after 9/11 in the United States. Then there was indeed a wave to gather everything. Save all the Internet interactions, because you never know what it's good for. In this context, I refer to the PRISM programme. The latter is not what we propose. We suggest that it should be related to a research assignment. The use of this bulk of information should be necessary for national security. You shouldn't be able to collect that information in any other way. That commitment must be proportionate to the goal you want to achieve. The question is, of course, what is proportional and how do we define it? In the first term, I was challenged to do so. We have already said on the cabinet side that listening to all telephone calls in a city in the Netherlands is no longer proportionate. That could serve any purpose. This might be interesting for any research and therefore not specifically for any research. And with that it is not research assignment related. If you would like to peel off exactly where the border is, I think you're falling into the trap of making the law again technologically dependent, which means that you can limit the years that we can continue with it very much. Then we are going to fill in this whole technique again, and I come to talk about things that are on the human body, and that seems unwise to me. There should be no misunderstanding that it should be really related to a research assignment. The services, which are also very handsome lawyers who help to complete an assignment, are ultimately burdened with a draft burden, a task assigned to them by one of the two ministers. That minister is there himself. That minister, who works on the basis of the trust of the House of Representatives, therefore we are sitting here assessing whether the assignment is indeed proportionate and can then have it carried out. That body checks whether the investigation is indeed necessary and proportionate. Moreover, it remains the case that the supervisor can see what practice is emerging. On the basis of this, she can say about a specific study: we see it as such, but I think it is just the example of the coffee cups that I have just mentioned, which gives you too broad an explanation of the legal limits that we see. Well, then that's a big advice. Let me answer in one breath the question about the role of Parliament. The question of how this is done is indeed interesting. I think that is what Mrs Schouten and Mrs Tellegen were asking for. What scenarios are there? Suppose the minister does something and the supervisor says the minister shouldn't. The supervisor then produces a public report, often, or always, with a secret attachment for the CIVD, which then knows exactly all the details. Then you actually have two options. It is possible that will usually be the practice that the minister says: we have heard you correctly, we stop or we do it differently, in a different way, because the supervisor has spoken. However, it may theoretically also be that the Minister says: yes, but I am responsible for national security, and I think this is of the utmost importance, and I believe that this is a matter of legality as it stands in the current law, and I am confronting Parliament with it. That would be confidential section of parliament, the CIVD. It may be that Minister says he has seen the supervisory report, but he believes that this should be done in relation to national security and that it is also legitimate that it should be done. If then the parliament, so the CIVD, says that the minister should not do so, he should still commit or resign. In this case, I think that would mean saying to the president of the CIVD: I can't say why. If the equivalent of a majority in the CIVD loses faith in the minister, he must of course resign there. I think those are the scenarios. Parliament's control is therefore complete, but it is to some extent a matter of confidentiality. It could therefore in theory have never happened that the minister in the CIVD loses the confidence of a majority of the Chamber and then has to quit.

I will go to the individual speakers in a moment, but I would like to move on to my fourth block: international cooperation. No speaker disputes that this is necessary. In fact, on many occasions there is a critical question as to why this cooperation is not more intensive. As far as the AIVD section is concerned, we are working closely with the services in the surrounding countries, but also with the United States and with countries around the world in the field of counter-terrorism. That happens in different degrees. I'll get back to you on that. In the case of military missions, the Minister of Defense can tell you more about this. The whole operation is often carried out in cooperation with other countries. The same applies to the information position which precedes it and which accompanies the mission. There are a number of locks on the door for foreign cooperation to prevent what happens that people sometimes worry about, namely that information from the Netherlands just ends up in a foreign service. Let me walk you through those locks. Firstly, only information that has been lawfully collected may be shared with a foreign service. The fear that a service abroad indicates that it wants certain information, after which that information is provided, is not correct. It can't be. You can only provide what you have and you can only have what you have rightfully collected. That is what we have just discussed: it must be necessary and proportionate. Secondly, an assessment shall be made prior to the sharing of information. Who do we share this information with? The law lays down that "new" "records" that there will be a weighting analysis of all the countries in the world with which we want to share information. That's a job and it needs to be done carefully. That is why we are taking some time to do so. That's the second lock on the door. Thirdly, it is also new in this law................................................................................................................................................................................................ When I arrived, I was surprised to have to impose a burden on an individual tap on a telephone number, while the sharing of bulk information was now only about the bulk information collected via the ether and could take place at the level of the service, without the Minister knowing about it. I have now set up this procedure through my office, but we are now going to lay down in the law that this will require a burden from the Minister in the future. Fourthly, the supervisor shall be informed immediately. If it feels that something is being shared that should not be shared, it can of course always ring the bell through the trajectory we have just discussed. We have provided as many guarantees as possible for the sharing of information with foreign services. But at the same time we face the fact that we can only work together with foreign services, in some cases even intensively. In this context, Mr Amhaouch asked whether the data abroad is subject to Dutch jurisdiction. That is an important point for him. Let me go into this in detail. If the services store data outside the Dutch legal sphere, this is contrary to their duty of care. That obligation alone stems from the obligations of care laid down in the law. For example, look at Articles 23 and 24. This includes the provision of the necessary technical and organisational facilities for the security of data processing against loss or deterioration as well as unauthorised data processing. This means that the services cannot store that data on a foreign server, because they cannot guarantee that unauthorised persons, such as foreign services, will not have access to it. Those obligations also apply to Defence, if the acquisition takes place within the framework of its own mission. Another situation is the one I was just talking about. If information is shared with those four locks on the door, it falls under Dutch jurisdiction. In doing so, it has been given to the partner with whom we work on that point. So much for my fourth block on international cooperation.

ParlaMint-NL_2017-02-08-tweedekamer-10.u319	But the tricky thing about that is: what's useful? It's very hard to tell.
