ParlaMint-NL_2017-02-08-tweedekamer-10.u320	First of all, how do you know it's really necessary? Firstly, it is of course the services and the minister responsible who assess whether it is still useful to collect this information in the context of the duty of care. There is also a confidence element in that. In addition, the supervisor is always able to plow through it and say: wait a minute, this is kept longer than is strictly necessary; this practice is not good. The supervisor may address this in general terms in the public report and, if necessary, with underlying information in the secret section to the CIVD. I come to the last of the six general issues: the role of the supervisor and the review committee. In this context, I would also like to discuss Mr Recourt's question as to whether there are any remaining opinions on the current legislative proposal from the CTIVD that have not been taken over by us. How do these two relate to each other? At an earlier stage we have considered whether it would not be a good idea if the organisation that has been set up to do this kind of business, which is already bound by secrecy and which has experts in the house, organizes the independent consent in advance? That was also the opinion of the CTIVD at the time. In the end, this was not considered desirable, as the supervisor would have to supervise a route in which he himself gave his consent, in which he himself has a role to play. We have deliberately separated that from each other. On the basis of the rulings of the European Court of Justice, we had to organise independent prior authorisation at the Hague Court in any case, in terms of source protection of journalists and attorney-client interactions. At that time we looked at each other deeply and found that it was wise to organise an independent test in advance, also looking at the development of case law. At the same time, of course, we have looked at our services and said: can we organise it in such a way that it remains workable? You need to be able to act fast. For this reason, the present form has been chosen. In the opinion of the Cabinet, this form certainly meets the requirements of the ECHR. It is an independent ex ante test in which the TIB assesses whether the statement of reasons can bear the decision: is it right, on the basis of the information provided, that the Minister has given the order to the service or has given it permission? The TIB can always say that it finds it incompletely motivated. For this reason, she may want more information. If she doesn't get that information, she can decide not to agree, and then it stops there. That process can continue for as long as the TIB finds it useful and necessary. The TIB can always ask questions. According to Article 36 of the new law, it can ask for the Minister's support to be able to do the work even better. How does this relate to ministerial responsibility? Look, the minister has to act within the legal framework that the TIB is part of. You can't break the law. If the TIB says it is not allowed to do so, then you are bound by it as a minister, but there is full room for political assessment by the House of Representatives. I'll give you a scenario. The Minister approves what he or she himself considers necessary for national security and which is subsidiary to other means and which is proportionate. He is then corrected by a TIB, who says: no, this is not allowed. I think it would be wise if the Minister didn't say, "Well, then we don't do anything." No, I can very well imagine that the Minister thinks: hearing the TIB's judgment, I must try to serve national security in a different way and I must come up with a different burden, partly, better argued or thrown over another bow. If he does not do that well, he can be held fully liable by Parliament in accordance with normal political rules. So that TIB should never be an excuse for the Minister's failure to act. The Minister can say at some point that he intended to, but that it proved not to be legitimate on further examination and that he did something else at that time. That's what he's responsible for.

ParlaMint-NL_2017-02-08-tweedekamer-10.u329	I come to the question about the relationship with the CTIVD. The CTIVD is the regulator on the entire route of the activities of the security services. That's not just after permission has been given. That impression is sometimes given. The supervisor may also monitor the trajectory of the burden formation. Is the Minister sufficiently informed when he gives such a burden? In this context, it can look at the activities that take place with a helicopter view. That includes the TIB. Again: I am making it up here, so I have to be careful with that, but it may be that the supervisor thinks that the burden is generally based on information that is not good, or that they could be better motivated. That is, of course, a relevant advice for the person who gives the burden. Will I first answer Mr Recourt's question as to what views of the CTIVD we have not yet incorporated into the law? When it comes to monitoring the activities of the service, it is about Roma locuta, causal finita. If the supervisor has spoken and he has said that something should not be done because it is unlawful, then you must come as a minister of very good house to do it anyway. Now, however, we are talking about the legislative process and then we are taking over the opinions to a large extent, but there may be something else that we see in our legislative role. There are indeed a few examples. Not much, because we have accepted most of the points. We have not taken over the distinction in retention period for content and metadata, if only because in our view the difference between metadata, traffic data and content is sometimes unclear. This is the case for e-mails, for example, because they sometimes send content messages in the subject field, which is strictly part of the metadata. We do that ourselves sometimes when we send an e-mail. The concept of metadata does not seem to me to be a useful concept for the retention periods. That is why we have set one deadline for everything. We have included the duty of care, as advised by the CTIVD, but we have less implemented it. The CTIVD suggested that we would oblige the service to establish the written data, the description of each carrier. We didn't want to lay that down with that fineness in the law. This also applies to the alignment with the TIB. It might be useful for the CTIVD and the TIB to speak with each other, but we should be careful that we do not strive under the heading of "right unit" for those two to always coordinate what happens, because then one will become the boss of the other. That is not the relationship we would wish for. We didn't take over that part. Each has its own role in this order. Furthermore, the law does speak for itself in the end. I do not know if it is an exhaustive list, but I thought I had sufficiently motivated it. When I discussed this subject, I have already given a slightly different coloration of the journalistic sources than perhaps the opinion of the CTIVD, but I have explained that in substance. I think I have made a number of points. There may be a few more, but that also follows from the law, in which we finally arranged it differently.

ParlaMint-NL_2017-02-08-tweedekamer-10.u357	Please continue.
ParlaMint-NL_2017-02-08-tweedekamer-10.u358	Nothing has changed in terms of intercepting packages. This relates to the mode of operation of the service, so we do not provide information in public. In general, I can say that the services naturally take certain measures so that the recipient does not become aware of any delay. That was and remains the case on the basis of the new law. It is asked whether cable access is needed for attribution research, to see where the hacks come from. Yes, cable access will contribute significantly to the attribution research. This is the short version of the answer. It has also been asked how wrong people are prevented from coming to the attention of the services as a result of the adoption of other people's identities by targets. On this point, too, nothing changes compared to the current law. The services are aware that targets can assume a different identity. They are also aware that it can be the identity of an unsuspecting citizen. By analysing and correlated the data to data already collected and to be collected, the services are able to determine whether the identity of an unsuspecting citizen is being abused. If this is established, the data already collected from the citizen concerned will be removed from the systems of the relevant service and destroyed. That's what happened before. A question has also been asked about the algorithms used. By letter of amendment, I included a duty of care which requires the heads of service to take care of the necessary facilities to promote the accuracy and completeness of the data processed and to promote the quality of data processing, including the algorithms and models used. I cannot give more openness to the algorithms that the services use, because that would provide an insight into their way of working. In the case of automated data analysis, how is the context of the data taken into account? The short answer is: as far as possible, that happens. In itself, researching metadata for the services is not new, because they are already doing so via the ether. According to the locational climate, I believe that this was also stated in the report's note, a business impact assessment was carried out in order to assess the impact on business. It is clear that the impact on business will be very limited. By the way, companies also have an interest in what is called "a safe place to do business," so in the end it is also good for companies that the services do that. That's about the economic disadvantage. This will eventually be taken into account when it comes to proportionality. In principle, services do not carry out an economic test. However, if it had total disproportionate effects, it would be part of the proportionality test. Can a service refuse to cooperate on a request for information referred to in Article 39? The answer is yes. That hasn't changed from the current law either. They asked about hacking and what is a "significant vulnerability." That's what we've been talking about before. It is clear that the services do not oblige people there to have legal basis for....................................................................................................................................................................................................................................................... That is not the intention, quite the contrary. The service urges people to try to remove vulnerabilities from systems, because they may pose security risks. Another thing is that the service can naturally encounter a vulnerability. Then, in the interests of security, before the person concerned is informed that he has a vulnerability in his system, it can be used to test for national security purposes. For this purpose, the question is relevant as to whether this vulnerability is a result of negligence, whether it is already known to the public, and how much damage occurs if that vulnerability is not immediately reported. This must then be balanced against the importance of identifying, for example, a source of vulnerability, identifying a terrorist and gathering information in this way. The more significant the vulnerability, the larger the group for whom that vulnerability is relevant, and the less it can be imagined that these reasons can be invoked. There were questions about filtering large datasets. That must be done as quickly as possible in the process. This can be positive and also negative. I'm not going to describe that in detail right now. How is it determined which fiber is needed for cable connections? Article 52 of the bill provides for a power for the services to obtain the necessary information from a provider. When there is a reasonable expectation that the necessary data flow is present on a particular fiber or fibre, the services request permission to intercept a specific data flow for a short time. That goes by the minister in question and also along the TIB, who then has to give the consent. The technical and substantive characteristics will then be used to determine whether that flow of data is actually relevant to those research assignments. That is the competence of Article 49 in the legislative proposal. We have already talked about selectivity, also in response to questions from the VVD. The question has also been asked by myself whether we should perhaps wait a little while and, in parallel with each other, reflect the constitutional change and this law. I would like to remove the suggestion that when we amend the Constitution, we will be left to decide what is necessary under the Intelligence and Security Services Act. I fully agree with Mr Verhoeven that the hierarchy is a reverse and that, of course, the Constitution precedes and provides the framework for the laws as we fill them in here. I think that is the answer to Mr Verhoeven's questions. Then I shall go on to answer Mr Van Raak's questions even more quickly. The legislative proposal provides for a legal basis for the exercise of reference at the request of third parties by Mr Van Raak. This means that the existing practice, as is known, inter alia, from the CTIVD's monitoring reports, is now regulated by law. That is another example of legal form. Where this had actually become the practice on the basis of CTIVD opinions, it is now also laid down in the law. I have already spoken about Mr Hirsch Ballin's statement. I put it in context, too. When it comes to giving a burden to impose special resources, politicians as such have no special protection other than that of the law. I can imagine that, as part of the proportionality test, special resources will not be used lightly. But that should also be the case when it comes to the bakery on the corner, so let me take that back. I believe that every citizen should have special resources only if it is necessary and proportionate. This also applies explicitly to politicians. However, the mere fact that you are alderman, or landed from a waterboard, or MP, does not mean that you can avoid the sight of the security services if there is a legitimate reason to use special means.

Mrs Tellegen asked about the importance. What can't happen? I have tried to illustrate this with an example, but in the end this is an important point. If we have any suspicion that someone is playing a role in threatening our national security, then we can use targeted means, but that is not enough. So we really need this in our repertoire, as do our neighbouring countries. Could you give permission for the three phases in one go? The answer is no. It is important that each of these three stages of acquisition, processing and analysis should be carried out sequentially, precisely in order to avoid mass data collection and to immediately look at the content. That is a concern that has rightly been raised here in the Chamber. You wouldn't want that. If people are looking for their computer at home because of any personal situation, they need to know that in Zoetermeer there is not someone watching what they are doing. That is precisely why I attach great importance to separating those stages of acquisition, processing and then specifically analysing the data, with the consent and supervision at each of those stages. A request was made for the capacity utilisation of the services. That will have to develop in practice. In the end, the integrated designation also plays a role. I have spoken about ministerial responsibility. There was a question about speed and care. If we set it up as we have done now, I think that the workability of the services that enable them to act effectively will be maintained, while there will be a substantial test by the TIB which also meets the requirements of European law. There's a sequence in there. In order to make pace, I would imagine that we would be able to inform the TIB in advance in the form of: "This burden will go to the Minister tomorrow, but here you have it. We don't know if the minister likes him so much, but you can take a look at it." ParlaMint-NL_2017-02-08-tweedekamer-10.u373	Mr. Chairman. The good news is that I can be very brief, because my colleague from the Interior has, of course, taken over most of the questions as captain. First of all, and perhaps a little bit in conclusion, let me say that nobody anticipated that the restriction we cycled in 2002 in the Wiv, namely that interception is only possible on non-cable networks, would lead fifteen years later to the largely blind and deafness of our intelligence services. But that is the reality we are talking about. Indeed, a very large proportion of telecommunications has moved from air to cable networks. In the first period, I was a little surprised at the fact that there was always talk of a new power. Fortunately, some of you corrected that, because that authority has been in place for a long time. The majority of communication has simply moved from the air to the cable. In the digital age in which we live now, with all our mobile means of communication and complex networks, it is not very difficult to conclude that the proceeds of so-called classical interception methods are falling incredibly fast. I have been asked what it means if we do not modernise the Wiv 2002. This means that Dutch soldiers on mission................................................................................................................................................................... I just got back from Mali. This is a very complex country, in which many groups are connected. Mobile devices are also widely used in Mali. Also for that we need this new Wiv not a bit fast, but very quickly. Failure to modernise the Wiv 2002 means that terrorist activities may not be recognised in good time, that the real intentions of countries at risk, for example in the field of weapons of mass destruction, remain hidden and that, in the event of rapidly emerging crises abroad, an adequate information position can be built up significantly less quickly. All this, of course, has to do with what I have just said twice, namely that communication has increasingly moved from the air to the cable. My colleague from the Interior has just indicated that the threats in cyberspace are absolutely real. They affect all of us: businesses, citizens, governments and institutions. We're all gonna get hit by it. The same goes for the DOD. The timely identification of cyber threats is essential for our security and for the cyber security of the Netherlands and Europe. The Netherlands has become a very important node of data. I refer, for example, to the Amsterdam Internet Exchange. In another context, I often talk about securing the flows, our supply lines, and that is one of them. It is also relevant here. The granting of cable access, as my colleague from the Interior rightly said, is of great importance to the Netherlands as a safe place to do business, a safe place to live, work and do business. I can give you many examples, but I have already summarized a few of them and I think they are enough to appeal to the imagination. By the way, STUD has certainly used the right arguments not to notify. However, it has been established that insufficient guidance or coordination has been carried out, that too limited capacity has been made available and that STUDY in general and in a broad sense lacked awareness of the obligation to notify. In other words, it has simply not been given sufficient priority. The CTIVD has rightly established that the reasonable period has been exceeded and that the process has not yet been automated. It can't be. When I was a member of the Chamber, I made this a big song in the case of the AIVD, so frankly it bothers me if the STUD is delayed too and does not give sufficient priority to it. All the recommendations of the CTIVD have been taken over and that means that STIVD is busy with those recommendations and thus the improvement process. That process has already started. The aim is to have completed the backlogs up to the year 2016 by the end of this year. At the beginning of 2018, the 2017 notification studies will be carried out, after which the new notification studies can be carried out in time in the course of 2018. In doing so, I have answered the questions to my address.

ParlaMint-NL_2017-02-08-tweedekamer-10.u388	Mr. Chairman. If a fisherman is told in the future that he is not allowed to use a towing net, I would advise him to call it a "research-oriented net." For this, historical data are indispensable. We are also very often longer present in operating areas. Think of Afghanistan; there we were ten years and walking. Mali; three years and running. Gulf of Aden; eight years and running. It shows that those retention periods have been considered. Once again, the Home Secretary has said it very well: this is a maximum.
